Where, How, and When: Query-Guided
Grounding for Video Understanding
A thesis submitted by
Yogesh Kumar
in partial fulfillment of the requirements for the award of the degree of
Doctor of Philosophy
under the supervision of
Dr. Anand Mishra
Indian Institute of Technology Jodhpur
Department of Computer Science and Engineering
October 2026
arXiv:2610.21422v1  [cs.CV]  15 Oct 2026


Abstract
Understanding video content requires reasoning across multiple levels of granularity, from
identifying individual objects to interpreting their relationships and localizing specific temporal
actions or moments. This thesis explores three distinct grounding tasks that correspond to
fundamental questions in query-guided video understanding: Where key objects are present
(object-level grounding), How these objects interact (relationship-level grounding), and When
specific actions occur (moment-level grounding). These tasks collectively enable a structured
interpretation of dynamic scenes in videos, progressing from fine-grained object localization to
coarse temporal localization.
For object-level grounding, we present QDETRv (Query-guided Detection Transformer for
Videos), a novel extension of Detection Transformer that enables one-shot localization of unseen
objects in videos. QDETRv employs (i) query-context fusion, where query features are integrated
with contextual video frames to enable effective temporal reasoning; (ii) memory module to
maintain temporal consistency across frames; and (iii) unsupervised pretraining strategy using
synthetic object trajectories with recurrent queries and feature-reconstruction objectives for a
better initialization, and thereby achieves substantial gains over competitive baselines. Taking it
forward, to address the limitations of QDETRv, we propose the Temporal Object-Aware Vision
Transformer (TOViT), which leverages a language-aligned, large-scale pre-trained open-set vision
encoder. This enhances semantic discrimination, enabling better separation of visually similar
objects through rich linguistic priors. Additionally, TOViT introduces a filtering mechanism
that propagates high-confidence object features across frames, allowing temporal consistency to
support the detection of small and partially visible objects.
For relationship-level grounding, we propose few-shot referring relationships in videos where
given a query visual relationship, i.e. (subject, predicate, object) and a target video, the task is to
localize the entities using only a few support videos sharing the same predicate. Our method
formulates this as an optimization over a T-partite random field, with candidate bounding
boxes as vertices across T frames. A relation network with query-conditioned translational
embeddings is trained via episodic meta-learning. Two aggregation techniques, Global Semantic
Aggregation (GSA) and Local Localization Aggregation (LLA), incorporate temporal context,
and belief propagation-based message passing produces subject-object trajectories. Experiments
show strong performance, effectively addressing the long-tail distribution of visual relationships.
For moment-level grounding, we propose Moment Alignment Transformer (MATR) for
video-to-video moment retrieval. MATR employs dual-stage Dynamic Time Warping for pre-
and post-fusion alignment within a transformer, capturing fine-grained temporal correlations.
Learnable query vectors guide moment localization via classification and boundary prediction
heads. A self-supervised pretraining strategy with temporal augmentations further enhances
ii


generalization. We further extended MATR to support text-to-video moment retrieval and
achieved comparable, if not better performance to the state-of-the-art methods.
In short, this thesis progressively advances the understanding of where, how, and when
in query-guided video comprehension by proposing a series of principled solutions spanning
spatial, relational, and temporal grounding. We believe that the ideas and frameworks presented
here will open up promising research avenues in various application domains such as sports
analytics, manufacturing, and skill understanding, among others.
iii


Acknowledgements
The completion of this doctoral dissertation represents not just a personal milestone but the
culmination of years of collaboration, support, and encouragement from numerous individuals
that have profoundly shaped my academic journey.
First and foremost, I extend my deepest gratitude to my PhD advisor, Dr. Anand Mishra,
whose guidance and mentorship have been essential in shaping both this research and my
development as a scholar. His pursuit of excellence, dedication to rigorous research methodology,
and promotion of innovative thinking have been a constant source of inspiration throughout
my doctoral journey. Dr. Mishra’s ability to balance constructive criticism with encouragement,
his open-door policy for discussions ranging from technical challenges to career guidance have
been invaluable. His hard work and dedication to maintaining the highest standards of research
quality continue to motivate me, and I am grateful for the excellent opportunities he provided
that allowed me to excel across multiple dimensions of academic research. I fondly remember
how, during my first publication, Dr. Mishra spent late night with me in the lab, patiently
guiding me through the process of clear academic writing, thoughtful experimental design,
and effective presentation of research. I am also thankful for his generous support for my
participation in national and international conferences, which greatly enriched my academic
exposure. Furthermore, his efforts in facilitating collaborations with industry partners provided
me with invaluable real-world insights and broadened the practical relevance of my research.
I am deeply grateful to my PhD committee members, Dr. Suman Kundu, Dr. Ravi Kiran, and
Dr. Angshuman Paul, for their thoughtful engagement with my research and their invaluable
suggestions that helped refine this work. I extend special appreciation to Dr. Manish Gupta
for his consistent feedback and guidance during our weekly meetings and paper writing. I am
also thankful to Dr. Anutosh Maitra, Dr. Sowmya Rasipuram, and Roshni Ramnani for their
collaboration and constructive feedback during our weekly discussions.
This research would not have been possible without the financial support of our funding
agencies and industry collaborators. I acknowledge the University Grants Commission (UGC)
for providing the fellowship that supported my doctoral studies. I am particularly thankful to
Microsoft and Accenture for their research grants and collaborative partnerships that provided
computing resources for our lab and also offered valuable insights into real-world applications.
I extend my sincere appreciation to the faculty, staff, and administration of IIT Jodhpur for
creating an environment conducive to high-quality research and learning. The institution’s
commitment to enabling innovation and providing state-of-the-art research facilities has been
fundamental to the success of this work. I am particularly grateful for the technical support staff
(Rimpesh) who ensured that our computational infrastructure remained robust and accessible
iv


throughout the demanding phases of this research.
My doctoral journey has been greatly enriched by the opportunity to work alongside talented
students and research associates in our lab. I have been fortunate to collaborate with several
undergraduate and research associates, including Prajwal, Saswat, Shreya, Abu, Kranti, and
Uday. The doctoral journey, with its inevitable challenges and moments of uncertainty, has
been made more manageable and enjoyable through the friendship with my fellow graduate
students. I am grateful to my friends, fellow graduates and lab-mates, Abhirama, Rishab,
Rekhansh, Vishnu, Kartik, Surbhi, Somraj, Abhishek, Niveddita, Neelu, Akshita, Satyam, Prince,
Pushkar, and Anoop who have been my companions through both the challenging and successful
moments of this journey. Our discussions, ranging from technical debates to philosophical
conversations about research and life, have provided both intellectual stimulation and emotional
support.
Perhaps most importantly, I owe my deepest gratitude to my family, whose support, sacrifice,
and belief in my aspirations have been the foundation upon which this achievement stands. My
brother deserves special recognition for shouldering additional family responsibilities, allowing
me to focus entirely on my studies. My mother and sister have been pillars of emotional support
throughout this journey, providing comfort during difficult times and celebrating each milestone
along the way. Their unconditional love, patience during my long absences, and faith in my
abilities have sustained me through the challenging phases of this work.
I also wish to acknowledge my young nieces, Pankhudi and Miradhya, whose joyful presence
during family visits provided much needed respite from the intensity of research work. Their
innocent curiosity and their ability to bring laughter and lightness to family gatherings have
been precious gifts that reminded me of the importance of maintaining balance in life.
At this point, I also wish to remember my late father, whose teachings continue to guide me
even in his absence. His belief in never losing hope and his lessons on perseverance during
difficult times have been a constant source of strength throughout my doctoral journey. The
values he instilled in me have not only shaped my character but also carried me through the
most challenging phases of my life.
Finally, I acknowledge that this doctoral dissertation represents not just my individual effort,
but the collective contribution of all these individuals and institutions. While my name appears
on the cover, the ideas, insights, and innovations presented within these pages have been shaped
by many interactions, discussions, and collaborations. Any errors or shortcomings in this work
remain entirely my responsibility, while its contributions to the field of computer vision and
video understanding reflect the collective wisdom and support of this remarkable community
of scholars, friends, and family members who have accompanied me on this transformative
journey.
Yogesh Kumar

1
Introduction
The digital era has witnessed explosive growth in the generation of video data. Recent studies
show that video accounts for more than 80% of global internet traffic [1], with platforms like
YouTube receiving more than 20 million uploads per day1. Surveillance systems generate
petabytes of footage daily [2], while billions of smartphone users continuously create and share
videos. This massive volume of visual data presents unprecedented opportunities for insight in
domains such as sports analytics, manufacturing, skill understanding, healthcare, education,
security, and scientific research. However, this abundance also presents major challenges. The
sheer scale of video content far exceeds what humans can view and analyze manually. To enable
scalable and timely understanding, there is a pressing need for automatic tools that can flexibly
interpret video content, adapt to novel scenarios, and extract user-relevant insights with minimal
supervision.
Crucially, such tools must go beyond generic summarization and support targeted video
understanding, where users seek specific insights into particular tasks or events rather than
exhaustive analysis of entire videos. For instance, in the sports domain, one may wish to identify
a specific object such as a player, a football, or a goalpost; an interaction like a player kicking
the football; or a moment/action such as a shot on goal or a successful save. These examples
underscore the need for query-based grounding systems that can parse dynamic gameplay on
demand and retrieve user-defined information with minimal supervision and effort.
In this thesis we study query-based grounding tasks and introduce methods that enable
flexible and targeted interpretation of video content with minimal supervision, even in open-
world settings. Toward this end, the goal of this thesis is illustrated in Figure 1.1. Consider
a video of a football match. Understanding the unfolding play requires addressing three
core questions while handling potentially unseen players, formations, or gameplay scenarios.
Specifically: (Objective (i). Understanding where) identifying key entities such as players, the
ball, and goalposts, even when player appearances, jerseys, or camera perspectives differ from
training examples. This objective is achieved through query-guided object localization using
visual queries; (Objective (ii). Understanding how) recognizing interactions such as “player
kicking the ball”, even when the interaction patterns or strategies differ from those observed
during training, which is addressed through relationship grounding using visual-relationship
queries; and (Objective (iii). Understanding when) detecting key temporal events such as a
“goal" even when the exact action instance or context was not seen during training, handled via
video moment retrieval using video queries.
This query-driven formulation enables the system to interpret video content based on

Figure 1.1:
Thesis Objectives. This work aims to advance query-guided video understanding by
addressing three core questions: where objects are present, how they interact, and when specific actions
occur. Consider a football match: understanding the play requires (i) identifying key entities such as the
ball, goalposts, and the players (where important objects are), (ii) recognizing interactions like “player
kicking the ball to a teammate" (how objects interact), and (iii) localizing key events such “goa" moment
(when critical actions occur). Together, these tasks enable structured interpretation of complex, fast-paced
sports scenes, moving beyond frame-level recognition toward temporally grounded, interaction-aware
understanding.
user-defined interests, eliminating the need to manually view entire videos. Each task is
designed to handle novel visual elements that may not have been encountered during training,
ensuring adaptability in open-worldd settings. Collectively, the three grounding tasks, object-
level, relationship-level, and moment-level, capture the multidimensional nature of video
understanding: objects serve as the foundational elements of scenes, relationships encode
meaningful interactions that define activities, and temporal grounding pinpoints when key
events unfold. By tackling these objectives through targeted queries, our approach empowers
machines to retrieve specific user-relevant information from unstructured videos with minimal
supervision. This provides scalable and user-centric video interpretation systems that flexibly
generalize to unseen concepts and deliver actionable insights in dynamic environments.
The following sections present a discussion of the key challenges, prior work, and our core
contributions.
2


1.1 CHALLENGES
While addressing the proposed-task towards query-guided video understanding we tackled
following challenges.
1.1.1 THE SCALABILITY CHALLENGE
Many video understanding methods face a major limitation: they rely heavily on detailed
annotations for every possible scenario. Traditional supervised approaches demand large-scale
training datasets that cover all object types [3], relationships [4], actions, and moments [5] that
may arise during deployment or test time making them difficult to scale in real-world settings.
This challenge is particularly pronounced when systems are expected to generalize from
limited supervision. In many practical cases, models must detect unfamiliar objects, interpret
novel interactions, or localize complex actions using only a handful of examples. For instance,
identifying an unexpected road obstacle, understanding a rare interaction like “plane flying
above truck,” or retrieving fine-grain actions such as “bicycle kicks” from a footwall match
video. The annotation burden becomes even more severe due to the compositional nature of
visual understanding, where the number of possible object-relationship combinations grows
exponentially.
To address the scalability challenge, the chapters 3, 4, and 5 focus on methods that can be
learned with one or a few examples. Instead of relying on exhaustive annotations, we develop
methods to generalize from one or a few examples, allowing scalable models. This shift from
annotation-heavy pipelines to adaptive learning frameworks provides a step toward building
practical, flexible video AI systems.
1.1.2 OPEN-SET GENERALIZATION
Real-world environments are inherently open-set: new objects, interactions, and events frequently
emerge without prior labels or limited training instances. Systems designed under closed-set
assumptions, where all categories are known in advance, struggle to adapt, especially in dynamic
domains like autonomous driving, surveillance, and content retrieval. This mismatch between
training and deployment distributions creates a critical need for open-set generalization, where
models must recognize and reason about novel concepts at test time.
In video understanding, this challenge is amplified by the temporal and compositional
complexity of visual scenes. Unlike static images, videos require consistent interpretation
across frames, making it harder to detect and track unseen categories, relationships, or actions.
Traditional pipelines with fixed category heads or frame-wise processing often fail to maintain
temporal coherence or calibrate unknowns. Recent open-vocabulary approaches [6, 7] leverage
language-aligned encoders to extend recognition to novel classes, but typically lack object-aware
temporal modeling, resulting in fragmented or inconsistent detections for unseen concepts.
To address these limitations, chapters 3, 4, and 6 propose query-conditioned formulations
3


that enable open-set generalization in video tasks. Our methods are designed to adapt for
unseen examples at test time while leveraging temporal structure for robust grounding.
1.1.3 BEYOND STATIC ANALYSIS
Moving from static image understanding to video analysis introduces a new dimension of
complexity, time. Unlike images, videos contain evolving scenes where objects move, interact,
and change appearance across frames. This temporal axis brings several challenges; models
must handle motion blur, occlusions, deformations, and viewpoint shifts while maintaining
consistent predictions over time. Frame-wise methods that ignore temporal context often suffer
from fragmented tracks and unstable boundaries, especially when objects are briefly missed or
partially visible.
Effective video understanding requires spatiotemporal reasoning: systems must integrate
information across frames to preserve continuity and suppress noise. For localization and
detection, temporal fusion and cross-attention mechanisms help propagate high-confidence
signals and maintain object identity. For temporal action/moment grounding, precise temporal
alignment is essential, must match query and target sequences despite variations in speed,
duration, and execution style, placing accurate start–end boundaries rather than relying on
isolated frame-level matches.
To address these challenges, our work incorporates video-specific architectural designs that
go beyond per-image processing. We introduce temporal cross-attention modules, memory-
based fusion strategies (chapter 3), and sequence-level alignment objectives (chapter 6) that
enable robust spatio-temporal localizations and retrieval across time. These components allow
our models to maintain coherence, adapt to dynamic content, and deliver temporally consistent
predictions, advancing video understanding beyond static analysis.
1.2 PRIOR WORKS
1.2.1 OBJECT DETECTION AND GROUNDING
Object detection and localization in images have evolved from traditional CNN-based approaches
like Faster R-CNN [8], which use region proposal networks for candidate generation, and
YOLO [9], which formulates detection as a single-stage regression problem for real-time
performance, to transformer-based methods like DETR [10] that treat detection as a set prediction
task. These methods typically rely on predefined object categories and extensive supervised
training on large datasets [8, 9]. Query-based object localization represents a paradigm shift,
using visual queries such as single images of target objects to localize instances in target scenes.
Methods like CoAE [11] employ co-attention mechanisms to establish correspondences between
query images and target regions, while OS2D [12] provides versatile one-stage detection, enabling
detection of novel object categories without extensive retraining.
Extending object detection from images to videos introduces significant additional challenges
4


beyond static image detection. The temporal nature of videos creates complexities, including
appearance variations due to illumination changes, motion blur, viewpoint shifts, and occlusions
where objects disappear and reappear across frames. Videos require temporal consistency to
maintain coherent object identities and smooth trajectories across consecutive frames. Many
existing methods process frames independently or use simple temporal aggregation that fails to
capture complex object dynamics effectively [13, 14]. In this thesis (chapter 3), we developed
a query-guided detection framework [15] specifically designed for video object localization
using image queries, incorporating cross-attention mechanisms that capture information from
neighboring frames to enable robust localization while maintaining temporal consistency.
1.2.2 VISUAL RELATIONSHIP DETECTION AND GROUNDING
Visual relationship detection in images focuses on understanding interactions between objects by
identifying subject-predicate-object triplets such as “person riding bicycle" or “bird flying above
car". Early approaches concentrated on spatial relationships and human-object interactions,
with scene graphs introduced to represent multiple visual relationships in structured formats.
Traditional methods [16, 17] rely on strong supervision with extensive annotations for every
possible subject-predicate-object combination, limiting scalability to diverse relationship types.
Query-based relationship grounding [18], particularly referring relationships, represents a
more flexible paradigm where systems must localize subject and object entities given specific
relationship queries, typically using iterative message-passing mechanisms and language priors.
Extending visual relationship detection and grounding from images to videos introduces
substantial temporal complexities beyond static image analysis [4, 19]. Videos contain dynamic
relationships that evolve over time, such as temporal predicates like “chasing" or “following"
that have limited understanding in static images. Objects involved in relationships undergo
appearance changes, occlusions, and motion patterns that must be tracked consistently across
frames, while maintaining relationship consistency to avoid temporal discontinuities. Recent
video relationship detection methods have incorporated transformer architectures and temporal
attention mechanisms [19], but most struggle with novel relationship types not seen during
training. In this thesis (chapter 5), we address few-shot referring relationships in videos, where
the model localizes subject and object entities corresponding to unseen relationship queries
using only a few support examples, formulating this as an optimization problem over a temporal
random field with belief propagation.
1.2.3 VIDEO ACTION OR MOMENT GROUNDING
Video moment retrieval majorly focused on text-based approaches that locate temporal segments
in videos matching natural language descriptions [20, 21]. Early methods employed CNN-based
video encoders combined with RNN-based text encoders [22, 23], using simple alignment
mechanisms to match textual queries with video content. These approaches often struggled with
complex semantic understanding and temporal reasoning, relying on basic feature concatenation
or attention mechanisms. The introduction of transformer architectures revolutionized this field,
with methods like Moment-DETR [20], QD-DETR [21], and UniVTG [24] leveraging self-attention
and cross-attention mechanisms to achieve better alignment between text queries and video
5


segments, significantly improving localization accuracy through end-to-end learning.
However, text-based approaches face inherent limitations when describing complex visual
moments, temporal dynamics, or nuanced actions that are difficult to articulate precisely in
natural language. This challenge motivated the development of video-to-video moment retrieval
(Vid2VidMR), where actual video clips serve as queries instead of text descriptions. Video-
based approaches like GDP [23], FFI+SRM [22], and SRL [25] enable more intuitive querying
by allowing users to specify target moments using videos, particularly valuable for complex
activities that are hard to describe verbally. These methods face unique challenges in aligning
video sequences and capturing temporal correlations between query and target videos, requiring
sophisticated temporal modeling to handle variations in motion, appearance, and timing. Our
work (chapter 6) [26, 27] has explored dual-stage sequence alignment, self-supervised pre-
training objectives, and transformer-based architectures specifically designed for video-to-video
alignment to improve performance in this challenging task.
1.2.4 TRANSFORMER-BASED VIDEO UNDERSTANDING
Transformer architectures [28] were originally introduced for natural language processing, utiliz-
ing self-attention mechanisms to model long-range dependencies without recurrent connections.
Their success in NLP motivated adaptation to computer vision, starting with Vision Transformer
(ViT) [29] for image classification by treating image patches as tokens. DETR [10] further
revolutionized object detection by formulating it as a set prediction problem using transformer
encoder-decoder architectures, eliminating hand-crafted components like non-maximum sup-
pression. Adapting transformers for video understanding introduced unique temporal modeling
challenges beyond static image analysis, requiring mechanisms to capture relationships across
frames while maintaining computational efficiency.
Video-specific transformer variants [21, 20] incorporated spatiotemporal attention mecha-
nisms and cross-attention to align information between different temporal segments or between
query and target video content. Methods like Trans-VOD [30] addressed video challenges
by designing attention patterns that capture both short-term motion and long-term temporal
dependencies while handling variable video lengths. Vision-language transformers pretrained
on large-scale image-text datasets, such as Video-OWLViT [7], a CLIP-based model, have proven
particularly effective for video tasks requiring semantic understanding of novel concepts, trans-
ferring rich semantic knowledge for few-shot and open-vocabulary understanding. In this thesis,
we leverage transformer architectures through cross-attention mechanisms that capture temporal
context (chapter 3) from neighboring frames, dual-stage sequence alignment for video-to-video
matching (chapter 6), and query-guided attention that enables detection and localization of
novel objects or relationships using minimal supervision.
6

1.3 THESIS OBJECTIVES AND CONTRIBUTIONS
1.3.1 OBJECTIVE-1
Understanding “Where”: Develop a query-guided approach for object grounding that operates
in open-set settings using one or a few visual examples.
Query-Guided Object Grounding. Given a visual query 𝑞(e.g., an image or visual embedding of
an object) and a target video 𝑉= { 𝑓1, 𝑓2, . . . , 𝑓𝑇} consisting of 𝑇frames, the goal of query-guided
object localization is to determine a set of spatio-temporal regions
ℬ= {𝑏𝑖
𝑡| 𝑏𝑖
𝑡= (𝑥𝑖
𝑡, 𝑦𝑖
𝑡, 𝑤𝑖
𝑡, ℎ𝑖
𝑡), 𝑡(∈[1, 𝑇] ∪𝜙)}𝑛
𝑖=1,
that correspond to the visual entity represented by 𝑞, where 𝑛represents the number of instances
of the query object. Formally, the task can be viewed as learning a function
𝑓where : (𝑞, 𝑉) →ℬ,
where 𝑓where localizes the queried object across frames as shown in Figure 1.2.
Unlike conventional object grounding systems limited to a fixed set of categories, our
aim is to identify any visual entity specified through a query, thereby enabling open-world
generalization. The problem becomes particularly challenging in videos, where objects exhibit
temporal dynamics, occlusions, and appearance variations, requiring consistent localization
across frames with little to no task-specific supervision.
To address this, we study the problem in two related settings: one-shot and few-shot video
object localization. In the one-shot setting, the model receives a single query image and localizes
the corresponding object in the video. In contrast, the few-shot setting provides a small support
set of examples that guide the localization of novel object instances.
For the one-shot scenario, we introduce QDETRv (Query-Guided DETR for Videos) [15]
(chapter 3), a transformer-based architecture that extends DETR with temporal modeling
7

Figure 1.3: Given a query visual relationship as <subject, predicate, object> and a test video, our goal is
to localize the subject and object on the test video with the help of a support set containing a few videos
with the same predicate. In this example, the goal is to spatio-temporally localize the plane (subject) and
person (object) that are connected via fly above (predicate), with the help of a support set containing only
four videos sharing the predicate fly above. It should be noted here that fly above is unseen during training.
We refer to this problem as few-shot referring relationship in videos.
capabilities. QDETRv employs (i) query-context fusion, where query features are integrated
with contextual video frames to enable effective temporal reasoning; (ii) memory module to
maintain temporal consistency across frames; and (iii) unsupervised pretraining strategy using
synthetic object trajectories with recurrent queries and feature-reconstruction objectives, and
thereby achieves substantial gains over competitive baselines. Taking it forward, we extended
QDETRv for few-shot object grounding.
Building on this, we proposed a temporal object-aware Vision Transformer [31] (chapter 4)
that addresses QDETRv’s limitations by leveraging language aligned large-scale pre-trained
open-set detectors. It integrates semantic knowledge from vision-language models like OWL-ViT
to enable effective knowledge transfer and robust object generalization across time. The method
introduces a filtering mechanism that propagates high-confidence object features across frames,
enabling efficient progression and reduced noise. It integrates feature interaction between
current frames and past detections to maintain temporal coherence without explicit object-
tube proposals. Parallel classification and detection heads are optimized for few-shot settings,
achieving state-of-the-art performance.
Together, these contributions advance the understanding of “where” objects are located in
videos, establishing a robust foundation for open-world and query-driven object grounding
under limited supervision.
1.3.2 OBJECTIVE-2
Understanding “How”: Develop a query-guided method to ground unseen object interactions
(visual relationships) spatio-temporally using few examples.
Query-Guided Relationship Grounding. Given a visual relationship query 𝑞= ⟨𝑠, 𝑝, 𝑜⟩, where
8


𝑠and 𝑜denote subject and object visual entities, and 𝑝represents a predicate describing their
interaction, and a target video 𝑉= { 𝑓1, 𝑓2, . . . , 𝑓𝑇} consisting of 𝑇frames, the goal of relationship
grounding is to identify spatio-temporal trajectories
ℛ= {(𝑏𝑠
𝑡𝑖, 𝑏𝑜
𝑡𝑖) | 𝑡∈([1, 𝑇] ∪𝜙)}𝑛
𝑖=1
such that the localized regions correspond to entities engaged in interaction 𝑝, where 𝑛is the
number of instances of the query visual relation. Formally, the task can be defined as learning a
mapping
𝑓how : (𝑞, 𝑉) →ℛ,
where 𝑓how grounds relationships in videos by jointly reasoning over spatial and temporal
dependencies of entities, as illustrated in Figure 1.3.
While object localization answers “where” entities are, understanding “how” they interact
provides a richer, more compositional understanding of dynamic scenes. For example, the
relationships “person near bicycle” and “person riding bicycle” represent semantically distinct
interactions despite involving the same entities. Modeling such relational semantics is crucial
for high-level reasoning and event understanding. However, existing relationship detection
methods rely on exhaustive annotation of subject-predicate-object triplets [4], which severely
limits scalability and generalization in realistic, open-world video settings. In videos, these
challenges are further amplified by temporal evolution, occlusions, and discontinuities, as
relationships appear, vanish, and transform over time.
To address these challenges, we propose the task of few-shot referring relationship grounding
in videos [32] (5), where the goal is to ground novel subject-object interactions with limited
supervision. The problem is formulated as an optimization over a 𝑇-partite random field, where
𝑇denotes the number of frames in the video. Each vertex represents a candidate subject-object
pair, and the objective function combines frame-level detection potentials with relationship
similarity potentials learned through episodic meta-training. This design allows the model to
generalize to unseen predicates while maintaining temporal coherence across frames.
Our approach introduces query-conditioned translational relationship embeddings that cap-
ture semantic interactions between entities in a structured embedding space. These embeddings
are enhanced using two complementary attention-based aggregation mechanisms: (i) Global
Semantic Aggregation (GSA), which fuses I3D features across all frames using learnable attention
weights to incorporate long-range contextual cues, and (ii) Local Localization Aggregation (LLA),
which focuses on spatial and ROI features from neighboring frames within a temporal window,
thereby addressing occlusions and partial visibility.
A meta-learned Relation Network computes similarity scores between support and query
relationships, enabling robust matching even for unseen predicates. The resulting optimization
is efficiently solved via belief propagation-based message passing on the random field, producing
consistent and temporally aligned subject–object trajectories.
Together, these contributions advance the understanding of “how” objects interact in videos,
bridging the gap between object-level detection and event-level reasoning, and enabling scalable
few-shot grounding of visual relationships under dynamic video conditions.
9


Output Segment
Query Video
Target Video
Figure 1.4:
Given a query video depicting an unseen moment or action, the task is to temporally localize
the corresponding moment or action within a target video.
1.3.3 OBJECTIVE-3
Understanding “When”: Develop an approach that, given a text or video query, can ground
unseen temporal events in videos.
Video Moment Grounding. Given a query video 𝑞= {𝑞1, 𝑞2, . . . , 𝑞𝑇𝑞} (text or a video) and a
target video 𝑉= {𝑣1, 𝑣2, . . . , 𝑣𝑇} consisting of 𝑇frames, the goal of video moment grounding is
to identify the temporal segment
𝜏= [𝑡𝑠, 𝑡𝑒] ⊆([1, 𝑇])
in 𝑉that is semantically most similar to 𝑄. Formally, the task can be expressed as learning a
function
𝑓when : (𝑞, 𝑉) →𝜏,
where 𝑓when aligns visual and temporal representations between 𝑄and 𝑉to retrieve the
corresponding moment as illustrated in Figure 1.4.
While the previous objectives addressed where objects appear and how they interact, this
objective focuses on understanding when key events occur within a video. Moment-level
grounding thus extends video understanding from spatial and relational reasoning to temporal
reasoning, pinpointing the exact time intervals corresponding to meaningful actions or events.
Traditional approaches typically employ text-based queries for temporal localization; however,
such methods often face limitations in descriptive precision, temporal ambiguity, and modality
mismatch, which hinder their ability to capture subtle visual cues such as motion dynamics,
execution speed, or style variations.
To overcome these limitations, we introduce the Moment Alignment Transformer (MATR)
for video-to-video moment retrieval (Vid2VidMR) [26, 27] (Chapter 6). In this formulation, a
short video clip serves as a visual query to locate semantically similar moments within a longer
target video. This visual-to-visual alignment paradigm leverages the full richness of motion
and appearance information, offering a more precise and fine-grained retrieval framework than
text-based approaches. Furthermore, we extended MATR to handle text queries and achieved
performance comparable to state-of-the-art methods.
10


MATR employs a dual-stage sequence alignment strategy within a transformer architecture
to model temporal dependencies between query and target sequences. Specifically, the method
integrates Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) at two levels: (i) pre-fusion alignment, which enhances
semantic correlation between query and target representations before transformer encoding,
and (ii) post-fusion alignment, which refines target video features after fusion for more accurate
temporal correspondence. This two-stage alignment enables MATR to capture both coarse-to-fine
temporal relationships across varying time scales.
The architecture combines encoder representations with decoder-refined features through
learnable query vectors that guide moment localization. Two prediction heads perform (i)
foreground–background classification and (ii) boundary regression to predict the temporal
boundaries (𝑡𝑠, 𝑡𝑒) of the retrieved segment. MATR is trained using a multi-objective loss that
jointly optimizes alignment cost, classification accuracy, and boundary precision.
To further enhance model initialization and temporal reasoning, we introduce a self-
supervised pretraining strategy, where MATR learns to localize randomly sampled clips within
the same video. This auxiliary task aligns closely with the main Vid2VidMR objective and
encourages robust temporal correspondence learning.
Together, these contributions advance the understanding of “when” events occur in videos,
enabling temporally grounded, query-driven retrieval of meaningful moments. This comple-
ments the spatial (where) and relational (how) understanding objectives, thereby completing a
holistic framework for query-guided video comprehension.
1.4 THESIS ORGANIZATION:
This thesis is organized as follows.
Chapter 2 provides a background of Transformer and Few-shot learning relevant to our work.
This chapter establishes the foundation and contextualizes our contributions by highlighting key
advancements and limitations in the field.
Chapter 3 presents a one-shot video object localization framework using Query-Guided DETR.
We extend DETR with cross-attention, where queries stem from visual exemplars and key-value
pairs encode spatio-temporal video features. The chapter introduces an unsupervised pre-
training strategy leveraging synthetic object trajectories, recurrent memory, feature reconstruction,
and temporal consistency. Key innovations include query-conditioned object tokens, multi-scale
temporal fusion, and differentiable trajectory generation, enabling efficient localization of unseen
objects across video sequences via selective attention. The work presented in this chapter has
been published in the following publication: Yogesh Kumar, Saswat Mallick, Anand Mishra,
Sowmya Rasipuram, Anutosh Maitra, Roshni Ramnani, QDETRv: Query-Guided DETR for
One-Shot Object Localization in Videos, In Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial
Intelligence (AAAI), pages 2831–2839, 2024 [15].
Chapter 4 introduces the Temporal Object-Aware Few-Shot Video Object Detection Trans-
former, which enhances object-level understanding by integrating OWL-ViT a large-scale
vision-language model with specialized temporal modeling. We propose a filtering mecha-
11


nism that selectively propagates high-confidence object features across frames using adaptive
thresholding and cross-attention between current frame embeddings and previously detected
object representations. The work presented in this chapter has been published in the following
publication: Yogesh Kumar, Anand Mishra, Temporal Object-Aware Vision Transformer for Few-Shot
Video Object Detection, In Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence (AAAI),
pages 5764–5772, 2026 [31].
In chapter 5 we proposed Few-Shot Referring Relationships that extends object level grounding
to relationship level grounding through our T-partite random field formulation with belief
propagation-based optimization. The chapter details query-conditioned translational relationship
embeddings, Global Semantic Aggregation (GSA) and Local Localization Aggregation (LLA)
mechanisms for enhanced relationship representations, and meta-learning frameworks using
episodic training. The work presented in this chapter has been published in the following
publication: Yogesh Kumar, Anand Mishra, Few-Shot Referring Relationships in Videos, In
Proceedings of IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR),
pages 2289–2298, 2023 [32].
Chapter 6 introduces MATR, a transformer-based model for Video-to-Video Moment Re-
trieval using dual-stage Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) for sequence alignment. It features
learnable queries, multi-scale encoder-decoder fusion, and self-supervised clip localization. Key
innovations include differentiable DTW, boundary-aware classification, and temporal augmen-
tations to improve generalization. The work presented in this chapter has been published in
the following publications: (i) Yogesh Kumar, Uday Agarwal, Manish Gupta, Anand Mishra,
Aligning Moments in Time using Video Queries, In Proceedings of International Conference on
Computer Vision (ICCV), pages 20215–20225, 2025 [26], (ii) Yogesh Kumar, Uday Agarwal,
Manish Gupta, Anand Mishra, Moment Alignment Transformer for Video-to-Video Moment Retrieval,
Computer Vision and Image Understanding (CVIU), article 104959, 2026 [27].
Chapter 7 concludes the thesis by summarizing key contributions, acknowledging current
limitations, and proposing future research directions in multi-granular video understanding. It
highlights opportunities to extend the presented frameworks toward more scalable, generalizable,
and interpretable video grounding systems.

2
Background
This chapter presents the foundational concepts and prior research that support the methods
proposed in this thesis. We highlight key prior works that inform or are directly integrated into
our contributions. Readers already familiar with these topics may choose to proceed directly to
the next chapter, where our core methodologies are introduced.
2.1 TRANSFORMERS
The Transformer [28] represents a paradigm shift in deep learning architectures, replacing
recurrent and convolutional operations with self-attention as the primary building block. Its
introduction was motivated by the limitations of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) [33] and
long short-term memory (LSTM) [34] models, which struggle with long-range dependencies
and suffer from sequential processing bottlenecks. In contrast, the Transformer processes entire
sequences in parallel and models pairwise interactions between tokens via attention, resulting
in superior scalability and performance.
Formally, given an input sequence x = (𝑥1, . . . , 𝑥𝑛), the Transformer produces contextualized
representations (ℎ1, . . . , ℎ𝑛) by allowing each token to attend to all others. The ability to capture
global dependencies without recurrence or convolution has led to state-of-the-art performance
across natural language processing (NLP) tasks such as machine translation, language modeling,
and text generation.
Despite its strengths, the Transformer faces key limitations. The computational and memory
cost of self-attention is 𝒪(𝑛2) with respect to sequence length 𝑛, making it expensive for long
inputs (e.g., documents, videos). Furthermore, unlike CNNs, Transformers lack strong inductive
biases such as locality and translation equivariance, requiring vast datasets for effective training.
Overfitting and instability in low-data regimes remain challenges, although pretraining on
massive corpora (e.g., BERT [35], GPT [36]) followed by fine-tuning has mitigated these issues in
practice.
The flexibility of the Transformer has inspired numerous domain-specific adaptations across
text, vision, multimodal, and video understanding. Table 2.1 provides a comparative summary
of representative Transformer models, highlighting their key innovations, targeted modalities,
and efficiency trade-offs.
13


2.1.1 TECHNICAL DETAILS
The Transformer consists of two major components: an encoder and a decoder, each composed of
a stack of identical layers [28]. The encoder is responsible for mapping an input sequence to a
contextualized representation, while the decoder autoregressively generates output tokens by
attending both to previously generated tokens and the encoder output.
At the heart of the architecture lies the scaled dot-product attention mechanism, which enables
each token to selectively focus on relevant parts of the input sequence. It operates on three
components: queries (𝑄), keys (𝐾), and values (𝑉), computed by projecting each input embedding
𝑥𝑖using learnable weight matrices 𝑊𝑄, 𝑊𝐾, and 𝑊𝑉. Intuitively, queries represent what each
token is looking for, keys encode what each token offers, and values carry the actual information
to be aggregated. The attention scores are computed by comparing queries against keys, and
these scores are used to weight the values:
Attention(𝑄, 𝐾, 𝑉) = softmax

𝑄𝐾⊤
√
𝑑𝑘

𝑉,
where 𝑑𝑘is the dimensionality of the keys. This formulation allows the model to dynamically
integrate context from the entire sequence, enhancing representation quality.
To improve expressiveness, multi-head attention (MHA) extends this mechanism by projecting
inputs into ℎdifferent subspaces, computing attention independently in each, and concatenating
the results:
MHA(𝑄, 𝐾, 𝑉) = Concat(head1, . . . , headℎ)𝑊𝑂,
head𝑖= Attention(𝑄𝑊𝑖

𝐾, 𝑉𝑊𝑖
𝑉).
This allows the model to capture diverse types of relationships simultaneously (e.g., syntactic
and semantic dependencies in language, or local and global structures in vision).
Each encoder block contains:
1. Multi-head self-attention, where every token attends to all others in the input.
2. A position-wise feed-forward network (FFN), consisting of two linear layers with nonlin-
earity (ReLU or GELU).
3. Residual connections and layer normalization applied around each sub-layer for stable
optimization.
Formally, for input 𝐻(𝑙) at layer 𝑙:
˜𝐻(𝑙) = LN(𝐻(𝑙) + MHA(𝐻(𝑙))),
𝐻(𝑙+1) = LN( ˜𝐻(𝑙) + FFN( ˜𝐻(𝑙))).
The decoder mirrors this structure, but includes two additional mechanisms:
• Masked self-attention, which prevents access to future tokens and ensures autoregressive
generation.
14


• Cross-attention, where queries come from the decoder and keys/values are taken from the
encoder output, enabling the decoder to condition its predictions on the source sequence.
Since self-attention is permutation-invariant, positional encodings are added to input
embeddings to encode sequence order. Vaswani et al. [28] introduced sinusoidal encodings:
𝑃𝐸(𝑝𝑜𝑠,2𝑖) = sin



,
𝑃𝐸(𝑝𝑜𝑠,2𝑖+1) = cos

𝑝𝑜𝑠
100002𝑖/𝑑

,
where 𝑝𝑜𝑠is the position index and 𝑑is embedding dimension. Later variants include learned
embeddings and rotary position encodings.
By stacking 𝐿layers of encoders and decoders, the Transformer scales to highly expressive
models. A task-specific output head (e.g., linear projection with softmax for classification,
autoregressive token generation for language modeling, bounding box regression for detection)
is applied on top of the final decoder or encoder outputs.
A key property of the Transformer is its modality-agnostic nature; as long as data can be
represented as a sequence of embeddings, the architecture can process it. This includes words in
a sentence, image patches, audio frames, or spatio-temporal video tokens. Thus, the Transformer
has evolved into a general-purpose architecture for sequence-to-sequence modeling across
diverse domains.
Despite these strengths, the quadratic cost 𝒪(𝑛2) in sequence length 𝑛remains a bottleneck
for long sequences. This has motivated research into sparse, linear, or hierarchical attention
mechanisms, such as Longformer [37], Performer [38], and hierarchical variants designed for
vision and video tasks.
2.1.2 EXTENSIONS TO VISION AND MULTIMODALITY
The extension of Transformers to vision was pioneered by Vision Transformer (ViT) [29], which
views an image as a sequence of patches rather than pixels. Specifically, an image 𝐼∈R𝐻×𝑊×𝐶is
divided into 𝑁non-overlapping patches of size 𝑃× 𝑃, each flattened and linearly projected into
an embedding:
𝑧𝑖= 𝐸(𝑥𝑖) + 𝑝𝑖,
𝑖= 1, . . . , 𝑁,
where 𝐸(·) is a learnable projection matrix, 𝑥𝑖∈R𝑃2𝐶is the flattened patch, and 𝑝𝑖is a positional
embedding that encodes spatial order. A learnable [CLS] token 𝑧cls is prepended to the sequence,
and its representation after the Transformer encoder is used for classification.
Thus, the
Transformer input is:
𝑍= [𝑧cls; 𝑥1𝐸; 𝑥2𝐸; . . . ; 𝑥𝑁𝐸] + 𝑃,
where 𝑃denotes positional encodings. ViT demonstrates that Transformers can match or surpass
CNNs when pretrained on sufficiently large datasets (e.g., JFT-300M [39]).
To adapt Transformers more effectively for vision, several innovations were introduced.
DeiT [40] showed that careful regularization and knowledge distillation from CNN teachers
can enable ViT training on smaller datasets such as ImageNet-1k. Swin Transformer [41]
15


introduced a hierarchical design by computing attention within shifted local windows and
merging patches across stages, which reintroduces locality and reduces complexity. CvT [42]
integrates convolutional projections into patch embedding and attention layers to recover
inductive bias. Positional encoding strategies were also adapted for 2D spatial data, ranging
from sine-cosine embeddings on a 2D grid to learnable embeddings and relative positional
biases. These design choices illustrate how structural priors (e.g., locality, hierarchy) are injected
back into the otherwise permutation-invariant Transformer.
Modality Adaptation.
Transformers require each modality to be expressed as a sequence of
tokens. Different modalities are adapted as follows:
Vision.
• Patch embedding: Instead of words, images are split into patches 𝑥𝑖∈R𝑃2𝐶and projected
into 𝑑-dimensional embeddings, analogous to NLP token embeddings.
• Class token: A learnable [CLS] token is prepended for classification tasks.
• Hierarchical variants: Swin, CvT, and others employ patch merging, local windows, and
convolutional tokenization to reintroduce inductive bias.
• Positional encoding: Essential to preserve 2D spatial order, typically via sine-cosine 2D
embeddings, learnable embeddings, or relative position bias.
Multimodality.
• Text: Standard subword tokenization (e.g., BPE) with embeddings plus positional encod-
ings.
• Image (CLIP): A ViT-style vision encoder produces patch embeddings, while a Transformer
text encoder handles subword tokens. Both are aligned with a contrastive loss:
ℒCLIP = −1

Õ

exp(⟨𝑣𝑖, 𝑡𝑖⟩/𝜏)
Í
𝑗exp(⟨𝑣𝑖, 𝑡𝑗⟩/𝜏) + log
exp(⟨𝑡𝑖, 𝑣𝑖⟩/𝜏)
Í
𝑗exp(⟨𝑡𝑖, 𝑣𝑗⟩/𝜏)

,
where 𝑣𝑖and 𝑡𝑖are vision and text embeddings, and 𝜏is a temperature parameter.
• Cross-attention (ViLBERT, LXMERT): Separate unimodal encoders produce features for
image regions and words. Cross-attention layers then exchange information, e.g.,
Attn(𝑄text, 𝐾vision, 𝑉vision).
• Unified tokenization (PaLI, Flamingo): All modalities are projected into a shared token space,
allowing a single Transformer to attend jointly across them. For video/audio, temporal
patches or spectrogram frames are tokenized in the same way as image patches.
Recent large-scale multimodal foundation models (e.g., Flamingo [43], PaLI [44]) demonstrate
that with unified tokenization and sufficient scale, Transformers can serve as a backbone for
diverse multimodal reasoning tasks.
16


Adapting Transformers to vision and multimodality involves three main components: (i)
modality-specific tokenization (e.g., image patches, spectrogram frames, video cubes), (ii)
positional encoding schemes to capture modality structure (1D for text, 2D for images, temporal
encodings for video), and (iii) interaction mechanisms such as self-attention, cross-attention,
or contrastive alignment. This flexibility makes the Transformer a unifying architecture across
diverse modalities.
2.1.3 TRANSFORMERS FOR OBJECT DETECTION
Transformers have been adapted to object detection through DETR (DEtection TRansformer) [10],
which formulates detection as a direct set prediction problem. Unlike anchor-based detectors
(e.g., Faster R-CNN [45], RetinaNet [46]), DETR uses a fixed set of learnable object queries, which
the Transformer decoder maps to bounding box coordinates and class labels. A CNN backbone
extracts image features, which are flattened and fed into the Transformer encoder. The decoder
cross-attends between object queries and encoder features, refining predictions iteratively.
The loss function combines a bipartite matching cost and a set-based prediction loss:
ℒ=
Õ
(𝑦, ˆ𝑦)∈𝜋∗
ℒ𝑐𝑙𝑠(𝑦, ˆ𝑦) + 𝜆ℒ𝑏𝑜𝑥(𝑦, ˆ𝑦),
where 𝜋∗denotes the optimal one-to-one assignment between predicted outputs ˆ𝑦and
ground-truth targets 𝑦. This assignment is computed using the Hungarian algorithm, which
minimizes a matching cost. Unlike traditional detectors that rely on heuristic post-processing (e.g.,
non-maximum suppression), this formulation treats object detection as a direct set prediction
problem, ensuring permutation-invariant supervision and eliminating duplicate predictions.
DETR eliminates the need for non-maximum suppression (NMS) and anchor design, but
requires longer training to converge. Deformable DETR [47] improves efficiency by restricting
attention to sparse spatial locations using learned offsets. Variants such as DAB-DETR [48] and
DN-DETR [49] further accelerate convergence and improve localization.
2.1.4 TRANSFORMERS FOR OPEN-WORLD AND OPEN-VOCABULARY DETECTION
Traditional object detectors are typically trained under a closed-world assumption, where the set
of object categories is fixed and fully annotated. However, real-world environments are open-
ended: models must detect known categories, localize novel or unseen classes, and continually
adapt to new semantic spaces. This motivates two closely related paradigms: open-world object
detection (OWOD) and open-vocabulary object detection (OVOD).
Problem Setup.
In OWOD, let 𝒞train denote the set of known classes during training, and
𝒞test = 𝒞train ∪𝒞novel represent test-time classes, where 𝒞novel are unseen. The model must: (i)
detect and classify 𝒞train, (ii) localize instances of 𝒞novel as “unknown,” and (iii) incrementally
integrate 𝒞novel with minimal supervision while avoiding catastrophic forgetting.

Table 2.1: Representative Transformer architectures across modalities and tasks. We summarize their
core innovations and modality scope.
In OVOD, the objective is to enable detectors to recognize categories beyond 𝒞train by
leveraging external semantic knowledge from language models or image-text pretraining,
without explicit bounding-box annotations for novel classes.
Transformer-Based Approaches in OWOD.
Transformers naturally support OWOD due to
their query-centric design and compatibility with multimodal supervision:
• DETR-style extensions: DETR [10] reformulates detection as set prediction with object
18


queries. OW-DETR [58] extends this idea with attention-driven pseudo-labeling, a novelty
classification head, and an objectness branch to separate unknowns from background.
Fast-OWDETR [59] reduces overhead via lightweight pseudo-label selection and improved
bounding-box refinement.
• Incremental learning: Open World DETR [60] introduces exemplar replay buffers, multi-
view consistency, and knowledge distillation to expand classification heads while mitigating
catastrophic forgetting. PROB [61] models objectness using Gaussian distributions in
embedding space, explicitly separating objectness from classification and facilitating
smoother class expansion.
• Language-supervised detection: MAVL [62] and related models replace fixed classifier
weights with CLIP-style text embeddings. This enables zero-shot transfer by aligning
region features with category names or natural language descriptions, bridging OWOD
and OVOD.
• Prompt-based adaptation: Lightweight prompt-tuning strategies allow frozen vision-
language backbones (e.g., CLIP [50], PaLI [44], Flamingo [43]) to adapt their detection
heads to new categories with minimal fine-tuning, improving scalability.
Transformer-Based Approaches in OVOD.
In parallel, OVOD methods focus on leveraging
vision-language pretraining and semantic supervision to enable vocabulary expansion without
explicit bounding-box annotations:
• Knowledge distillation: ViLD [63] distills knowledge from CLIP into detection heads via
dual-branch learning. HierKD [64] and LP-OVOD [65] extend this with loss refinements
and pseudo-labeling schemes to improve transferability.
• Region-text pretraining: OVR-CNN [66] aligns region features with text embeddings,
while GLIP [67] scales this to billions of image-text pairs with grounding supervision.
RO-ViT [68] augments positional embeddings and replaces cross-entropy with focal loss
for better region-level discrimination.
• Prompt modeling: PromptDet [69] and DetPro [70] design structured or continuous
prompts to inject semantic priors. Context grading and background-aware prompts further
refine alignment with novel categories.
OWOD vs. OVOD.
While both paradigms extend closed-set detection, their emphases differ.
OVOD leverages language and large-scale pretraining to broaden the detectable vocabulary
in a zero- or few-shot fashion. OWOD additionally requires incremental adaptation: localizing
unknowns, integrating them as novel known classes, and preserving prior knowledge. Thus,
OVOD focuses on semantic expansion via language grounding, whereas OWOD addresses the
dual challenges of unknown discovery and continual learning.
19


2.1.5 TRANSFORMERS FOR VIDEO UNDERSTANDING
Extending Transformers to video understanding requires modeling spatio-temporal dependen-
cies efficiently. A straightforward approach is to tokenize video frames into spatio-temporal
patches, but the resulting sequence length grows quadratically with both spatial and temporal
resolution.
TimeSformer [52] addresses this by factorizing attention into temporal and spatial dimensions,
reducing complexity from 𝒪((𝐻𝑊𝑇)2) to 𝒪(𝑇2 + (𝐻𝑊)2). ViViT [53] explores several variants,
including factorized encoder design and spatio-temporal pooling. Video Swin Transformer [54]
adapts shifted window attention to 3D, producing hierarchical spatio-temporal representations.
For action detection and temporal localization, ActionFormer [71] uses a Transformer encoder
to aggregate long temporal context efficiently. VideoBERT [72] adapts BERT-style masked
prediction to video-text data, leveraging large-scale pretraining (e.g., HowTo100M [73]). Tube-
DETR [19] extends DETR-style architectures to spatio-temporal video grounding by introducing
a dual-stream video-text encoder and a space-time transformer decoder. It avoids handcrafted
proposals by using time-aligned queries and achieves efficient end-to-end grounding with
reduced memory footprint.
Key challenges for video Transformers include: (1) handling long temporal data, (2) high
memory/computation cost, (3) scarcity of labeled video data, and (4) robustness to variable frame
rates and motion. Common implementation strategies involve using CLIP [50], 2D CNNs (e.g.,
ResNet [74], Swin [41]) for spatial encoding, followed by a temporal Transformer for sequence
modeling. End-to-end video Transformers are increasingly feasible with advances in hardware
and efficient attention mechanisms (e.g., sparse, linear, or axial attention).
Overall, Transformers have become central to video understanding, powering state-of-the-art
results in classification, detection, retrieval, and captioning tasks, though scalability and efficiency
remain open research directions.
2.2 FEW-SHOT LEARNING
Few-shot learning (FSL) addresses the fundamental challenge of enabling models to generalize
to novel categories or tasks when only a handful of labeled examples are available [75]. Formally,
let 𝒞train and 𝒞test denote disjoint sets of base and novel classes, respectively. A model is trained
on a large-scale dataset 𝒟train = {(𝑥𝑖, 𝑦𝑖)}𝑁
𝑖=1, with 𝑦𝑖∈𝒞train, and evaluated on 𝒟test, where each
class 𝑐∈𝒞test is represented by only 𝐾labeled support samples 𝒮𝑐= {(𝑥𝑗, 𝑦𝑗)}𝐾
𝑗=1. The learner
must predict labels for a query set 𝒬drawn from 𝒞test. The typical setting is 𝐾≪|𝒞train|, with
𝐾= 1 (one-shot) or 𝐾= 5 being the most common.
While the FSL framework naturally extends beyond classification to tasks such as object de-
tection, segmentation, reinforcement learning, or cross-modal reasoning (e.g., vision–language),
in this section we primarily focus on the classification setting. This restriction allows for a clearer
comparison of methods under a standardized benchmark, while acknowledging that many
techniques have broader applicability.
20


In practice, FSL is often framed as an 𝑁-way 𝐾-shot classification problem: given 𝑁novel
classes and 𝐾support samples per class, the task is to predict the label of queries from those 𝑁
classes. Training usually follows an episodic paradigm [76], where each training episode simulates
a few-shot task by sampling 𝑁classes and 𝐾supports per class from 𝒞train, encouraging the
model to generalize to 𝒞test.
The motivation for FSL arises from real-world scenarios where collecting large annotated
datasets is infeasible, expensive, or even impossible. For example, rare diseases in medical
imaging may have only a handful of expert-annotated scans, while autonomous driving must
handle corner cases (e.g., animals crossing or unusual weather conditions) with extremely limited
data. In natural language processing, low-resource languages often lack parallel corpora. Unlike
conventional supervised learning, where abundant data supports statistical generalization, FSL
emphasizes inductive bias, transfer of prior knowledge, and meta-level learning to overcome the data
scarcity bottleneck.
2.2.1 APPROACHES
FSL research has produced multiple families of methods, each motivated by different perspectives
on how humans generalize from few examples. These are summarized in Table 2.2, and expanded
below.
(i) Metric-based approaches. These methods learn an embedding function 𝑓𝜃: 𝒳→R𝑑
that maps inputs into a metric space, where 𝒳is the input space and 𝑑is the embedding
dimension.
Classification is then performed by comparing query embeddings with class

Siamese networks [77] pioneered pairwise similarity learning, while Matching Networks [76]
used attention over support examples. Relation Networks [78] introduced non-linear metric
learning. More recent methods like Meta-Baseline [79] integrate metric learning with pretrained
backbones. These methods are computationally efficient and interpretable but degrade under
domain shift when support and query distributions differ.
(ii) Optimization-based approaches. In this family, few-shot learning is framed as meta-
learning across a distribution of tasks 𝒯∼𝑝(𝒯). Each task 𝒯is split into a support set 𝒮(used for
adaptation) and a query set 𝒬(used for evaluation). The key idea is to learn model parameters 𝜃
that can be quickly adapted to a task-specific parameter set 𝜃′ using only 𝒮, while still achieving
good generalization on 𝒬.
For example, Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) [81] learns an initialization 𝜃that

where 𝛼is the adaptation step size and ℒ𝒮is the task-specific loss computed on the support set
𝒮. The meta-objective is then to minimize the query loss after adaptation:
min
𝜃


