,
where ℒ𝒬denotes the loss evaluated on the query set 𝒬. This formulation explicitly enforces
that the learned initialization 𝜃is such that, after fine-tuning on 𝒮, the adapted model 𝑓𝜃′
performs well on unseen samples 𝒬from the same task.
Variants include Reptile [82], which simplifies the meta-update by averaging task-specific
updates; ANIL [84], which adapts only the final classification head; and ProtoMAML [85],
which integrates prototypes into the MAML framework. Latent Embedding Optimization
(LEO) [99] further improves scalability by learning low-dimensional latent codes for each task.
While optimization-based approaches provide strong task-level generalization, they can be
computationally expensive and unstable in high-dimensional parameter spaces, requiring careful
design and regularization.
(iii) Data augmentation and generative approaches. Generative methods aim to enrich the
support set by synthesizing new examples. Early work used GANs [100] and VAEs [101] to
produce class-consistent samples. More recently, diffusion models [89] have been employed for
controlled generation. At the feature level, interpolation-based augmentation (e.g., Mixup) is
22


widely used. Given two embeddings 𝑓𝜃(𝑥𝑎) and 𝑓𝜃(𝑥𝑏), a mixed feature ˜𝑓is created as
˜𝑓= 𝜆𝑓𝜃(𝑥𝑎) + (1 −𝜆) 𝑓𝜃(𝑥𝑏),
𝜆∼Beta(𝛼, 𝛼),
where 𝛼is a hyperparameter controlling the interpolation. Recent advances leverage text-guided
models such as CLIP, enabling semantically controlled hallucination [90]. While these methods
increase diversity, they risk introducing unrealistic or biased samples.
(iv) Self-supervised and contrastive approaches.
Self-supervised pretraining aims to
learn transferable visual representations without requiring annotated labels, which can then
be adapted to few-shot learning tasks. A dominant paradigm is contrastive learning, where
augmented views of the same instance are treated as positives, and embeddings from other
instances serve as negatives. The objective encourages invariance to data augmentations while
enforcing instance discrimination. A widely used formulation is the InfoNCE loss:
ℒNCE = −log
exp( 𝑓(𝑥) · 𝑓(𝑥+)/𝜏)
Í
𝑥−exp( 𝑓(𝑥) · 𝑓(𝑥−)/𝜏),
where 𝑓(·) is the encoder, 𝑥+ is a positive (augmented) sample of 𝑥, {𝑥−} are negatives drawn
from the batch or memory bank, and 𝜏is a temperature hyperparameter. This contrastive setup
naturally aligns with few-shot settings by learning discriminative embeddings that generalize
well across tasks.
Instance-level contrastive learning. SimCLR [92] relies on large batch sizes to provide sufficient
negatives and employs strong augmentations (cropping, color jitter, blur) along with an MLP
projection head. MoCo [91] alleviates the need for large batches by maintaining a dynamic
memory queue of negatives and using a momentum encoder for stable representations. These
methods yield highly discriminative embeddings suitable for metric-based FSL methods.
Bootstrap and redundancy-reduction approaches. BYOL [93] removes explicit negatives by using
an online encoder and a momentum target encoder, where the online network learns to predict
the target’s representations. Despite lacking negatives, BYOL avoids representational collapse
by architectural asymmetry. Similarly, SimSiam [102] shows that predictor asymmetry alone
suffices. SwAV [95] departs from pairwise contrast by performing online clustering with a
"swapped prediction" loss, aligning assignments of different augmentations, thereby learning
more semantically structured embeddings.
Transformer-based SSL. Vision Transformer (ViT)-based self-supervised learning has emerged
as particularly powerful for transferring to FSL tasks. DINO [94] employs a self-distillation
framework, where a student network is trained to match the representations of a teacher network
under different augmentations. The teacher parameters are updated as an exponential moving
average (EMA) of the student parameters, ensuring stable training and preventing collapse. This
design enables alignment of both patch-level and global representations, capturing fine-grained
semantic correspondences.
Building on this idea, EsViT (Efficient Self-supervised Vision Transformers) [103] introduces
token-level contrastive learning, where individual patch tokens are contrasted across different
views in addition to global features. By encouraging local token consistency, EsViT enhances
the contextual modeling capability of ViTs, which is particularly valuable for open-set and
23


cross-domain few-shot learning. Overall, transformer-based SSL methods leverage contextual,
patch-level interactions to produce richer representations, narrowing the gap between pretraining
and the demands of real-world FSL.
(v) Transformer-based and prompt-oriented approaches. Transformers naturally model
support–query interactions. Few-shot Transformers [96] use cross-attention between support
and query embeddings for direct adaptation. Prompt-based methods, such as CoOp [97], learn
continuous text prompts that adapt pretrained vision–language models (e.g., CLIP) for FSL.
Large multimodal models like Flamingo [43] and GPT-4V [98] demonstrate few-shot reasoning
via in-context learning, unifying few-shot and zero-shot paradigms. However, these methods
remain sensitive to prompt design and require substantial computational resources.
Overall, the concepts reviewed in this chapter establish the technical foundations for our
thesis. We discussed how Transformers evolved into a unified architecture for text, vision, and
multimodal reasoning; how their query-based formulations enable object detection, open-world
recognition, and video understanding; and how few-shot learning provides mechanisms for
generalizing under limited supervision. These ideas directly underpin the design choices in
our subsequent chapters, where we leverage Transformer-based models and low-supervision
learning to develop effective methods for query-guided video understanding.
24


3
One-shot Object Grounding in Videos
In this chapter, we study one-shot video object localization problem that aims to localize
instances of unseen objects in the target video using a single query image of the object. Toward
addressing this challenging problem, we extend a popular and successful object detection
method, namely DETR (Detection Transformer), and introduce a novel approach – query-guided
detection transformer for videos (QDETRv). A distinctive feature of QDETRv is its capacity
to exploit information from the query image and spatio-temporal context of the target video,
which significantly aids in precisely pinpointing the desired object in the video. We incorporate
cross-attention mechanisms that capture temporal relationships across adjacent frames to handle
the dynamic context in videos effectively. Further, to ensure strong initialization for QDETRv,
we also introduce a novel unsupervised pretraining technique tailored to videos. This involves
training our model on synthetic object trajectories with an analogous objective as the query-
guided localization task. During this pretraining phase, we incorporate recurrent object queries
and loss functions that encourage accurate patch feature reconstruction. These additions enable
better temporal understanding and robust representation learning. Our experiments show
that the proposed model significantly outperforms the competitive baselines on two public
benchmarks, VidOR and ImageNet-VidVRD, extended for one-shot open-set localization tasks.
The subsequent sections present the introduction, a review of related work, the proposed
methodology, and a detailed analysis of results and discussions.
3.1 INTRODUCTION
The field of computer vision has long been engaged in the pursuit of localizing objects of interest
within videos. In the past, the primary emphasis has been localizing the specified object within a
single frame on the entire video [104]. Additionally, efforts have been made towards recognizing
individuals based on their facial features [105] or clothing characteristics [106]. In a more novel
direction, some research has explored using natural language or sketch-based queries to precisely
locate objects within images [6, 107, 32, 108, 109]. As visual comprehension continues to advance,
there is a need for localization methodologies that can effectively pinpoint unfamiliar objects
in the video that were not encountered during the training phase and unexpectedly appeared
during testing. This is particularly relevant in various application areas, including surveillance
systems and industrial automation. We illustrate our open-set one-shot object localization goal
in Figure 3.1.
To address the challenge posed by open-set one-shot object localization, our goal is to
develop a model that, once trained, can localize any object query that might arise unexpectedly
25


Query Image
Target Video
Input
Output
Figure 3.1: Given an unseen query image and a target video, the model is expected to localize all instances
of objects present in the query image on the target video. [Best viewed in color].
during testing. As noted in some of the recent works [11, 12], such a model should maintain its
class-agnostic nature even during the training process. Our requisites, distinct from approaches,
necessitate prior access to the list of category names during both training and testing [110, 111, 112].
To this end, we extend a popular and successful object detection method, namely DETR (Detection
Transformer) [10], and introduce a novel query-guided detection transformer for videos –
QDETRv. The QDETRv leverages the visual representation of the query image to better localize
the target object in the video frames. To handle the temporal context in videos, we incorporate
cross-attention that captures information from neighboring frames of the video. The key and
value matrices of the cross-attention are obtained from the contextual and target frames, and the
query matrix is derived from the query image. The cross-attention operation produces the final
context-aware feature representation, which inputs the query-guided DETR. To ensure stronger
initialization of QDETRv for our targeted task, we introduced an unsupervised, video-specific
pretraining approach. During the pretraining phase, we employed a feature reconstruction loss
combined with recurrent object queries to enhance representation quality and temporal learning.
To facilitate our study, we have extended two existing datasets, VidOR [113] and ImageNet-
VidVRD [114], by splitting them into train and test sets such that there are no common object
categories between the two sets. We obtain query images from each object category from the
Google open-images [115] dataset. This dataset preparation ensures a realistic evaluation of
one-shot learning performance on videos containing unseen objects. Here, we must highlight that
we employ category-wise splits to conduct experiments. However, our model remains entirely
class-agnostic during the training and testing phases. Our experimental results demonstrate
the effectiveness of our proposed one-shot video object localization approach on the extended
VidOR [113] and ImageNet-VidVRD [114] datasets. By combining the query-guided DETR
with the video-specific context module, our method exhibits robust performance in localizing
instances of unseen objects in target videos.
The contributions of this chapter are twofold: (i) We proposed a novel category-free and
query-guided extension to DETR. Our proposed model – QDETRv incorporates a temporal
module, capitalizing on temporal context for superior object localization within videos. (ii)
We introduced a novel video-specific unsupervised pretraining for QDETRv. This pretraining
objective is analogous to our downstream one-shot localization task, resulting in a notable
improvement in the localization performance.
26


3.2 RELATED WORK
3.2.1 ONE/FEW-SHOT OBJECT DETECTION
Over the last few years, there has been significant progress in one/few-shot object detection,
largely due to the adoption of sophisticated strategies such as attention mechanisms, transformers,
and few-shot learning techniques [116, 117, 118, 119, 120, 110]. These strategies have been
successful in a variety of domains, encompassing both image-based and video-based object
detection, thus broadening the scope and application of these advanced detection methods.
Recently, CoAE [11] and OS2D [12] have shown significant advancements toward one-shot
object detection in images. The CoAE [11] makes ingenious use of co-attention and co-excitation
mechanisms to generate region proposals and highlight correlated feature channels, thereby
improving the accuracy and efficiency of object detection. On the other hand, the OS2D [12]
model stands out as a versatile one-stage system that simultaneously performs localization
and recognition tasks, proving its effectiveness in various detection scenarios. In the realm of
few-shot object detection, recent innovations have resulted in the development of models like the
feature reweighting-based model [119], the Multi-level Feature Enhancement (MFE) model [118],
the Counting-DETR [121] model for few-shot object counting and detection, and the Fast
Hierarchical Learning [122] model designed to address the catastrophic forgetting issue. These
models, each exploring different aspects of few-shot learning, have contributed to improved
performance across various datasets and settings. In the context of video object detection,
models like the Tube Proposal Network with Temporal Matching Network [121] and the Thaw
method [123] for few-shot learning in video object detection have been proposed. Both models
harness the power of attention mechanisms, transformers, and few-shot learning techniques to
achieve compelling results. There have been several successful advances towards developing
DETR [10] variants in recent years for addressing different objectives; for example, in [124],
authors proposed an incremental DETR that can generalize to novel classes with finetuning on
a few examples. In [125] proposed different variants of object query, and [125] proposed an
auxiliary one-to-many matching branch during training to enhance the performance of DETR
for object detection in images. In [126] proposed UP-DETR method that enhances the original
DETR [10] model by introducing unsupervised pretraining with a random query patch detection
pretext task, demonstrating considerable performance improvement. The advancements in
these areas suggest that the continuous refinement of these techniques can lead to substantial
improvements in object detection across image and video domains with limited supervision.
3.2.2 TRANSFORMER-BASED PRETRAINING
Pretraining methods based on transformers, such as BERT [35] and GPT [127] for natural
language processing, and ViT [29] for computer vision, have revolutionized the landscape of
various tasks, including object detection. These methods leverage self-attention mechanisms
to learn high-level semantic features from large-scale data, resulting in models with robust
generalization capabilities. By pretraining on large-scale data with diverse modalities, such
as image-text pairs [6], these models can enhance the quality of representation learning, and
also enable the transfer of this knowledge to downstream tasks, even when object-level data
are scarce, thereby circumventing some of the limitations of traditional methods. Furthermore,

unsupervised pretraining techniques, such as the random query patch detection proposed
by UP-DETR [126], have shown promise in enhancing the performance of object detection
models. By capitalizing on the strengths of the transformer architecture in capturing long-range
dependencies and spatial localization, these techniques offer exciting avenues for future research
and development in object detection.
3.3 QUERY-GUIDED DETR FOR VIDEOS (QDETRV)
A key motivation behind QDETRv is that one-shot video localization requires identifying unseen
objects from just a single query image, making it essential to exploit both the query’s visual cues
and the temporal structure of the video. QDETRv achieves this by integrating query-guided
cross-attention with frame-to-frame temporal context, enabling precise matching even when
the target object appears with variations across time. Moreover, its video-specific unsupervised
pretraining equips the model with strong spatio-temporal priors, allowing it to generalize beyond
seen categories. Together, these capabilities make QDETRv particularly effective for open-set
one-shot localization in videos.
Following, we discuss the formulation of the problem and architectural details.
Given a query image 𝐼𝑞featuring an unseen object, the objective is to spatiotemporally localize
all instances of the unseen object within a target video 𝑉. Videos, with their intricate interplay
of spatial and temporal dimensions, present both opportunities and challenges for localization.
Harnessing this complexity effectively ensures high precision and reduces false localization. To
this end, in this work, we proposed QDETRv that effectively harnesses the temporal context
from the neighboring frames for better localization of unseen objects.
In this work, we utilize a query image 𝐼𝑞, a frame 𝐼𝑡, and a set of adjacent frames 𝑆from the
target video. These frames are defined as 𝑆= {𝐼𝑡+𝑗}, where 𝑗= −𝑘𝑡𝑜𝑘with temporal context

the next frame as object queries for the decoder. Containing the semantic information extracted from

Feature Encoding: The query image, target, and contextual frames are processed through a
Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) encoder to generate corresponding feature representations.
This results in the dimensions 𝐼𝑞, 𝐼𝑡∈R𝐶𝑘×𝑀×𝑁and 𝑆∈R𝐶𝑡×𝑀×𝑁, where 𝑀and 𝑁are the height
and width of the CNN feature maps and 𝐶𝑘, and 𝐶𝑡are the channel dimensions.
Cross-Attention Mechanism for Temporal Context: In our work, we utilize the cross-attention
mechanism [28] to encode temporal context within the target frame and enable interaction
between the query image and the target frame. As part of this process, we first generate Query
𝑄∈R𝐶×𝑀𝑁, Key 𝐾∈R𝐶×𝑀𝑁, and Value 𝑉∈R𝐶×𝑀𝑁matrices using convolution operations of
kernel size 1 × 1. Subsequently, we apply the dot-product attention [28] between Query and Key,
which results in an attention map. The latter serves as a storage of correspondences between
the visual feature of the query image and the temporal context extracted from the video. This
attention map is then used to transform the target frame features represented by Value. This
process leads to output features 𝑂∈R𝐶×𝑀𝑁, which can be defined by the following equations:

3.3.1 UNSUPERVISED PRETRAINING OF QDETRV
Inspired by UP-DETR [126], we introduce a video-specific pretraining strategy suitable for
QDETRv. This pretraining strategy is designed to locate a specific query patch within each
frame, a process that aligns closely with our main objective. We devised a synthetic dataset
based on the UCF101 [128] dataset inspired by [129]. Our video-specific pretraining aims to
identify and locate the query patch in every video frame, mimicking our main video frame
localization task, as visualized in Figure 3.3. To ensure our pretraining approach is effective and
aligns well with our primary task, we have added two additional mechanisms: (i) We integrated
an unsupervised query reconstruction loss, as suggested in [126]. This additional loss improves
training signals and ensures high-quality embeddings are formed. The combined loss for this
pretraining phase is represented as:

predicting the patch in the subsequent frame, as depicted in Figure 3.3. To incorporate recurrent
queries, we used a memory module. The memory is a 256 × 100 matrix where 100 is the number
of object queries used in DETR, with each object query having a dimension of 256 × 1. During
pretraining, for every frame, it stores decoded object queries (recurrent queries) responsible for
localization. The recurrent queries carry temporal context from the previous frames and help to
localize the object in the subsequent frames. Their utility is quantitatively evaluated in Table 3.3.
We also incorporated an unsupervised image-level pretraining for UP-DETR [126]. During this
image-level pretraining phase, the primary objective is to localize a randomly selected patch
within the input image. For this pretraining stage, we employed the ImageNet [130] dataset.

3.4 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS
3.4.1 DATASETS AND PERFORMANCE MEASURE
In this work, we employed three primary datasets: VidOR [113], ImageNet-VidVRD [114], and
Open Images [115]. We further refined VidOR and ImageNet-VidCRD annotations, segregating
super-classes like fruits and vegetables. Extracted target frames from both datasets were paired
with Open Images. Some low-frequency classes were excluded during training, but all frames
were used in testing. Note that our method is class-agnostic and does not use the class information
during training and testing. Dataset statistics are provided in Table 3.1.
Dataset for Pretraining: For effective visual representation learning, synthetic datasets are
crucial given the challenging task of annotating real object movements in videos. We begin by
generating a simulated trajectory resembling object movement. Consistency is ensured using
bounding boxes at chosen keyframes, with in-between positions filled by linear interpolation.
Patches from real video frames are then superimposed on this trajectory. The UCF101 [128]
dataset, featuring 13,320 videos with diverse complexities, was employed for pretraining, given
its suitability to improve our approach.
Performance Measure: We have utilized the mean Average Precision (mAP) at an Intersection
over Union (IoU) of 0.5 as our primary evaluation metric. Our evaluation operates framewise,
considering each frame as a separate detection problem.
31


3.4.2 BASELINES AND IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
The absence of prior work on class-agnostic one-shot video-based object localization methods
inclines us to benchmark our proposed method against image-based techniques in a frame-wise
fashion. However, such models might not be able to capture temporal aspects of the video.
RETRIEVAL-BASED APPROACH
A naive approach would involve combining an object detector that detects all objects as one class
coupled with an image retrieval system for one-shot detection. This system utilizes the object
detector’s detections as a database and class objects as queries to search for relevant images.
Inspired by baselines in [12], we utilized object detectors with identical architectures.
COAE [11]
It uses non-local operations to explore the co-attention embodied in each query-target pair and
yield region proposals accounting for the one-shot situation. It then formulates a squeeze-and-
co-excitation that adaptively emphasizes correlated feature channels to help uncover relevant
proposals and, eventually, the target objects. This method intentionally casts the learning
formulation such that it does not solely rely on the label information of training data but instead
explores correlated evidence revealed by the query-target pairs.
OWL-VIT [6]
We used it as the one-shot image-conditioned object detection method for the video by applying
it at each frame. OWL-ViT used a Vision Transformer architecture and contrastive image-text
pretraining.
UP-DETR [126]
It refines the DETR model via unsupervised pretraining using a random query patch detection
task. By selecting random image patches as queries and training the model to detect them,
UP-DETR addresses challenges like balancing classification with localization by freezing the
CNN backbone and managing multiple query patches with attention masks. This approach
significantly boosts UP-DETR’s performance in one-shot object detection, leveraging pretraining
to achieve enhanced accuracy with minimal labeled data.
IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
We pre-train and fine-tune our models using the Adam optimizer [131] with an initial learning
rate = 1e-5. The initialization of our frame and query encoders leverages weights of ResNet-50

3.5 RESULTS AND DISCUSSIONS
The results presented in Table 3.2 showcase the performance comparison of various methods
applied for both of the datasets, Vid-OR [113] and VidVRD [114]. The methods assessed include
baselines Retrieval-Based [12], OWL-ViT [6], CoAE [11], UP-DETR [126] (QDETRv without
temporal module), and our QDETRv, with the latter two being tested both with and without
pretraining.
3.5.1 PERFORMANCE OF QDETRV
For the Vid-OR dataset, QDETRv with video pretraining yielded the best performance with
43.1% for seen and 41.6% for unseen classes, surpassing its performance without pretraining,
which scored 38.6% and 40.2%, respectively. It’s important to note that unsupervised video
pretraining improved the results significantly, highlighting its effectiveness as a strategy for this
task. We get similar observations for the ImageNet-VidVRD dataset.

Table 3.3: Ablation studies on recurrent queries during pretraining showed improved performance. This
highlights the importance of adding temporal context in the pretraining phase.
3.5.2 ABLATION STUDY ON TEMPORAL CONTEXT MODULE
To evaluate the effectiveness of the temporal context module integrated into QDETRv, we
conducted an ablation study where this module was removed. We exclusively adopted an image-
level approach for unsupervised pretraining, eliminating the recurrent queries and leveraging
the ImageNet [130] dataset. Without a context module with image-level pretraining, our method
becomes similar to UP-DETR [126]. The results from this ablation demonstrated a notable 3.2%
performance boost for QDETRv without pretraining. Furthermore, with pretraining, there was
a 1.4% enhancement in performance on VidOR [113] dataset for the unseen test set as shown in
Table 3.2. These findings confirm the significant contribution of the temporal context module to
the model’s efficacy, both in the presence and absence of pretraining.
3.5.3 FINE-TUNING WITH VARIED TEMPORAL CONTEXT WINDOWS
As we progressed to the fine-tuning stage, we explored different temporal context windows,
spanning a range from 3 to 9, as depicted in Figure 3.4. Interestingly, our results indicated
that as the temporal context window expanded, there was a corresponding enhancement
in performance. This trend underscores the pivotal role of temporal context in improving
localization accuracy. This accuracy curve should be read alongside its cost. For a window
of size 2𝑘+ 1, the cross-attention in the temporal context module is computed over the target
frame together with its 2𝑘context frames, so both the FLOPs and the activation memory of
that module grow linearly in the window size, while the encoder–decoder cost is unchanged.
Widening the window therefore buys accuracy at a linear price, and the practical ceiling is set
by memory rather than by any accuracy plateau. At the operating point we adopt, QDETRv
runs at 11 FPS with 7.6 GB of peak memory, benchmarked under identical conditions against
the methods reported in Chapter 4. That comparison also indicates the preferable direction for
deployment: rather than widening a fixed window, the framework of Chapter 4 propagates only
high-confidence object features across frames and is both faster (14 FPS) and more accurate for a
modest 0.5 GB of additional memory, so selective propagation dominates window widening.
3.5.4 CROSS-ATTENTION VISUALIZATION FOR VARIED TEMPORAL CONTEXT
Figure 3.5 shows the cross-attention visualization on the test frame for different window sizes.
The attention to the object of interest improves with an increase in the window size, showing the
efficacy of temporal context being utilized from context frames of the video.

3.5.5 ABLATION ON RECURRENT QUERIES
Table 3.3 presents an ablation study focusing on the impact of recurrent queries during the
pretraining phase. When recurrent queries are employed (indicated by ✓), there’s a noticeable
performance improvement on both datasets: 0.6% on ImageNet-VidVRD [114] and 1.3% on
VidOR [113], compared to when these queries are omitted (✗). While seemingly modest, these
increments emphasize the added value of recurrent queries in capturing intricate temporal
information during pretraining.
3.5.6 CLASS-WISE PERFORMANCE
Table 3.4 shows the class-wise performance on the VidOR dataset. Intriguingly, larger-sized
objects like bear, kangaroo, and leopard generally achieve better performance compared to
relatively smaller objects such as coconut and lemon. The model performs better on larger
objects because they occupy more pixels and provide stronger visual features, making them
easier to localize reliably. In contrast, small objects offer limited resolution and weaker feature
cues, which reduces detection accuracy.
A promising direction to mitigate this issue within our current framework is to incorporate
multi-scale attention or lightweight FPN-style feature fusion. By leveraging multi-level feature
maps with higher spatial granularity, the model can better preserve fine details and improve
localization of small objects.

3.5.8 FAILURE CASE ANALYSIS
While our proposed methodology has shown promising results, it does come with a few
limitations: our model QDETRv highly relies on the quality of the query image for object
localization in videos. As shown in Figure 3.6 (red box), occluded query images often lead to
weak or missing localization. Further, we observe the following major failure cases: (i) occlusion,
(ii) visually similar object, (iii) small object, and (iv) overlapping instances on randomly selected
video frames. Figure 3.7 shows a selection of these examples. We leave addressing these
limitations of the current model as the future scope of this work.
36


3.6 SUMMARY
In this chapter, we introduced the novel class-agnostic query-guided DETR for videos (QDETRv),
effectively enhancing video object localization using a query image and temporal context. We
showed evaluations on two extended datasets that displayed superior and consistent performance
of QDETRv, particularly when paired with video-specific unsupervised pretraining. While
showing promise, our approach has a few limitations, including limited success in localizing
small or occluded object instances. Further, it struggles to differentiate between visually similar
objects. We leave addressing these limitations as future work and firmly believe this work will
open up new research avenues in open-set one-shot video object localization.
In the following chapter, we generalize the one-shot object grounding paradigm to a few-shot
setting and introduce a novel framework.
37


4
Few-Shot Object Grounding in Videos
Few-shot Video Object Detection (FSVOD) addresses the challenge of detecting novel objects
in videos with limited labeled examples, overcoming the constraints of traditional detection
methods that require extensive training data. This task presents key challenges, including
maintaining temporal consistency across frames affected by occlusion and appearance variations,
and achieving novel object generalization without relying on complex region proposals, which
are often computationally expensive and require task-specific training. Our novel object-aware
temporal modeling approach addresses these challenges by incorporating a filtering mechanism
that selectively propagates high-confidence object features across frames. This enables efficient
feature progression, reduces noise accumulation, and enhances detection accuracy in a few-shot
setting. By utilizing few-shot trained detection and classification heads with focused feature
propagation, we achieve robust temporal consistency without depending on explicit object
tube proposals. Our approach achieves performance gains, with AP improvements of 3.7%
(FSVOD-500), 5.3% (FSYTV-40), 4.3% (VidOR), and 4.5% (VidVRD) in the 5-shot setting. Further
results demonstrate improvements in 1-shot, 3-shot, and 10-shot configurations.
The subsequent sections present the introduction, a review of related work, the proposed
methodology, and a detailed analysis of the results and discussions.
4.1 INTRODUCTION
Object detection in videos has witnessed significant progress with the advent of deep learning-
based methods in the last few years [132, 133, 123, 110]. However, most traditional methods focus
on closed-set detection, where models are trained on predefined object categories with extensive
labeled samples per category. This makes them impractical for many real-world applications
that require the detection of novel object categories with limited training samples. Few-Shot
Video Object Detection (FSVOD) [110] addresses this challenge by enabling models to detect
novel objects in videos using only a few examples as shown in Figure 4.1.
Unlike widely-studied few-shot object detection in images [124, 120, 119, 118, 116], FSVOD
must account for temporal consistency, motion blur, occlusion, and variations in object appearance
between frames, making the task substantially more difficult [110]. Existing approaches for
FSVOD [117, 134, 135, 15] face several limitations, such as they often process frames independently
without leveraging temporal information [117], struggle with false positives due to weak
temporal matching [110], use region or tube proposal networks not optimized for few-shot
scenarios [117, 110, 135], and have difficulties with visually-similar objects and occlusions [15].
38


Support Set
Target Video
…
…
Output
Input
Figure 4.1: Given a support set containing novel objects and a target video, the goal of FSVOD [110]
is to detect all the instances of novel objects on the target video. We propose object-aware temporally
consistent few-shot object detection framework that significantly improves state of the art for this task.
[Best viewed in color].
On the other hand, recent advances in vision-language models like Video OWL-ViT [7] have
demonstrated impressive capabilities in open-vocabulary video object detection by leveraging
CLIP’s vision-language pre-training. Video OWL-ViT can be naturally adapted for few-shot video
object detection by replacing text prompts with visual prompts from support images, enabling
the detection of novel objects using only a few examples. However, this adaptation faces a key
limitation, i.e., lack of object-aware temporal modeling, as it processes frames independently on
the detected objects, resulting in inconsistent object detection across frames.
Our approach leverages vision-language pretrained frame encoder to transfer semantic
knowledge from image-text data, enabling recognition of novel object categories. Furthermore,
contrastively-trained vision encoder is more effective in distinguishing between visually similar
objects across different categories and handles partially visible or occluded objects compared
to CNN backbones (like ResNet [74]) used in prior methods [110, 135, 15]. This robust feature
representation serves as the foundation for our temporal consistency mechanism. To enhance
this consistency, we fuse representations of previously detected objects with current frame
detections, preserving relevant temporal information and adapting to appearance changes over
time. Unlike methods that process frames independently, our approach maintains memory of
past observations for better temporal reasoning. Our detection is directly optimized to condition
on few-shot visual examples, providing better results compared to traditional region proposal
networks. Further, unlike conventional tracking-by-detection pipelines [110, 135], our end-to-end
learning of video-specific object representations leads to superior generalization.
In summary, in this chapter we make the following contributions: (i) We revisit the relatively
under-explored task of Few-Shot Video Object Detection (FSVOD) and introduce simple yet
effective task-specific innovations.
Our key contribution is a novel object-aware temporal
39


modeling approach, that selectively propagates high-confidence object features across frames.
This enables efficient feature progression, reducing noise accumulation, and improving detection
accuracy in few-shot video scenarios. (ii) For the first time, we adapt large-scale pre-trained
vision-language models for FSVOD, aligning with recent trends in open-world detection. This
integration enables novel object detection by leveraging broad visual-linguistic knowledge,
facilitating efficient adaptation and strong generalization to unseen categories. (iii) We perform
extensive evaluations against baselines and state-of-the-art methods on four public FSVOD
benchmarks. Our approach achieves significant performance gains, with AP improvements
of 3.7% (FSVOD-500), 5.3% (FSYTV-40), 4.3% (VidOR), and 4.5% (VidVRD) in the 5-shot
setting. Further, we observe consistent improvements across 1-shot, 3-shot, and 10-shot setups,
demonstrating robust generalization across different few-shot scenarios.
4.2 RELATED WORK
Few-Shot Object Detection: Recent advances in few-shot object detection (fsod) have been
driven by attention mechanisms, transformers, and meta-learning techniques. Approaches like
CoAE [11] and OS2D [12] have pushed the boundaries of one-shot object detection in images
through co-attention mechanisms and versatile one-stage approach, respectively. For traditional
fsod, methods such as feature reweighting [119], contrastive proposal encoding [116], and
hierarchical learning [122] have demonstrated improved performance across various benchmarks.
In addition, several DETR variants [124, 125, 126] have emerged and shown promising results
for few-shot object detection in images. Although fsod in images provides the foundational
techniques and can be trivially extended to videos by performing few-shot detection on frames
independently. Our experiments suggest that they often fall short. The inherent temporal nature
of videos introduces additional complexities that require specialized approaches to maintain
consistency across frames while preserving the few-shot learning paradigm. We introduce
object-aware temporal consistency to overcome the inherent challenges specific to video.
While few-shot object detection in images has been extensively studied, its video counterpart
remains relatively under explored. Qi et al. [110] have formally introduced a few-shot video
object detection task and accompanying datasets. They proposed a proposal-based approach,
where object detection trajectories are first proposed and later refined using the matching
network. Kumar et al. [15] have specifically studied this problem for one-shot settings using a
novel query-guided variant of DETR. More recently, Wentano et al. [135] enhanced temporal
information by context fusion. Our work addresses key limitations of existing FSVOD methods
by utilizing a transformer-based backbone (OWL-ViT) pre-trained on large-scale image-text
pairs, replacing the CNN-based backbones used in prior approaches and improves semantic
knowledge transfer. In addition, unlike proposal-based networks not optimized for few-shot
scenarios, our proposal-free method conditions detection directly on few-shot-trained heads
and improves generalization to novel object categories.
Open-World Object Detection: There is a fundamental shift in computer vision literature
toward detecting novel visual concepts driven by models pre-trained on large-scale vision and
language data [136, 137]. These large models help in detecting novel objects by leveraging their
broad visual and linguistic knowledge, efficient adaptation, and improved generalization to
unseen categories. Recent works like OWL-ViT [6] and Video OWL-ViT [7] leverage vision-
40


language models pretrained on large-scale data to detect open-vocabulary concepts. Methods
like GLIP [67] and OpenSeed [138] further advance this paradigm by incorporating grounded
pretraining and structured knowledge distillation. Open-world object detectors can be extended
for image-conditioned detection, enabling users to detect objects by providing a few visual
examples. In this work, we leverage OWL-ViT encoders for image-conditioned detection in a
few-shot setup and enhance the existing Video OWL-ViT [7] by introducing an object-aware
decoder that significantly improves temporal consistency across video frames, and thereby the
few-shot video object detection performance.
4.3 METHOD
Few-shot video object detection (FSVOD) aims to detect novel object categories that were not
encountered during training. The task involves a support set containing a few images of novel
object classes and a target video consisting of multiple frames. The goal is to identify and localize
all instances of the objects from the support set across every frame of the target video, as shown
in Figure 4.1.
4.3.1 PROBLEM FORMULATION
In the FSVOD task, we aim to locate and classify all instances of objects belonging to novel classes
in a target video using only a few support examples. Formally, given an 𝑁-way 𝐾-shot support
set 𝒮= {(I𝑖,𝑗, 𝑐𝑖)|𝑖= 1, ·, 𝑁; 𝑗= 1, ·, 𝐾} where I𝑖,𝑗represents the 𝑗-th support image of class 𝑐𝑖,
and a target video 𝒱= {F𝑡|𝑡= 1, ..., 𝑇} with 𝑇frames, our goal is to detect all instances of the 𝑁
novel classes across all frames. For each frame F𝑡, the model predicts a set of object bounding
boxes along with their class labels.
4.3.2 DESIGN MOTIVATION AND ARCHITECTURAL OVERVIEW
Our proposed approach is designed to address the following unique challenges associated with
FSVOD task: (i) the limited supervision requiring efficient knowledge transfer, (ii) the necessity
for temporal consistency across sequential frames despite appearance variations due to motion,
occlusion, and viewpoint shifts, and (iii) the open-set recognition capability to distinguish
between novel objects and background elements without explicit training.
To address these challenges, we propose an object-aware, temporally consistent few-shot
video object detection framework. Our approach consists of the following main components:
a language-aligned vision encoder that provides semantically rich visual representations, a
temporal fusion decoder that selectively propagates high-confidence object features across
frames and, few-shot matching and detections heads that aligns query frame features with
support examples. Figures 4.2,4.3 illustrates our overall framework.

Figure 4.2: Object-aware Temporal Consistency. We utilize the OWL-ViT encoder output tokens as queries
but, critically, only forward the matched object tokens (𝑐𝑝> 𝜏) as key-value pairs to the next frame’s
decoder. This mechanism selectively propagates high-confidence object features across frames. This
enables efficient feature progression, reducing noise accumulation. We empirically found a threshold (𝜏)
and compared it with class probability (𝑐𝑝) scores for selective propagation. This selective propagation
mechanism significantly reduces noise accumulation across frames and maintains focused, consistent
visual representations of detected objects throughout the video sequence.
4.3.3 OBJECT-AWARE TEMPORALLY CONSISTENT FSVOD FRAMEWORK
LANGUAGE-ALIGNED VISION ENCODER:
Traditional CNN backbones like ResNet [74], trained solely on visual classification tasks, often
struggle to generalize to novel classes with limited examples. To overcome this limitation, the
recent trend [136, 7] is to use a pretrained vision encoder that has been aligned with language
semantics through large-scale vision-language pretraining. We adopt this by utilizing an OWL-
ViT encoder pretrained on large-scale image-text pairs. This encoder transforms each input
image I into a grid of patch embeddings E ∈R𝑃×𝐷, where 𝑃is the number of patches and 𝐷is
the embedding dimension.
SUPPORT SET ENCODING:
(i) Support Feature Extraction. Given a support set 𝒮containing 𝐾examples for each of the
𝑁novel classes, we extract patch-level features from each support image. For each support
image I𝑖,𝑗, the encoder generates both patch embeddings E𝑖,𝑗∈R𝑃×𝐷, and their corresponding
objectness scores for each patch, s𝑖,𝑗∈R𝑃. A higher objectness score indicates a higher probability
of an object being present in that particular patch. To obtain an object-centric representation, we

where z𝑖,𝑗is the selected patch embedding that best represents the target object in the support
image. (ii) Support Prototype Generation. After extracting object-centric features (z𝑖,𝑗) from
each support image, we aggregate information from the 𝐾support examples to form a class
prototype. For each class 𝑐𝑖, the final class prototype z𝑖is computed by averaging across the 𝐾
selected object-centric features. This aggregation creates a robust representation for each novel
class that captures essential visual characteristics across multiple support examples, allowing
the model to handle intra-class variations more effectively.
OBJECT-AWARE TEMPORAL FRAME DECODER:
(i) Target Frame Feature Extraction. For each frame F𝑡in the target video, we extract patch
embeddings using the OWL-ViT image encoder, F𝑡∈R𝑃×𝐷. (ii) Temporal Fusion. To ensure
temporal consistency in object detection across frames, we propose a filtering mechanism that
43


selectively propagates high-confidence object features from one frame to the next. This approach
facilitates efficient feature progression and minimizes noise accumulation by leveraging the
embeddings of successfully detected objects from previous frames. By doing so, it enhances
temporal consistency and improves the overall accuracy of object detection in video sequences.
For the first frame (𝑡= 0), we process the frame embeddings directly. For subsequent frames
(𝑡> 0), we incorporate information from previous frame detections via a cross-frame interaction
that selectively integrates object-level features to enhance temporal consistency. Specifically,
we select embeddings from the few-shot classification head (V𝑐𝑙𝑠
𝑡) of the previous frame where
detection confidence exceeds a threshold 𝜏(shown in Figure 4.2):

while detecting objects in the current frame.
FEW-SHOT DETECTION HEADS:
The temporally enhanced frame embeddings ˆF𝑡are processed through parallel classification and
localization heads to produce the final detection results.
Classification Head. The classification head compares frame embeddings with support class
prototypes to determine object categories. It projects frame embeddings to the classification
space:

Each patch is assigned a probability distribution over the 𝑁classes and background using

Localization Head. The localization head predicts bounding box coordinates for detected objects

corresponding patch 𝑝. For each patch 𝑝, if its maximum classification score exceeds threshold
𝜅, we consider it as a valid detection.

Our model is trained end-to-end by optimizing a combined loss function across all video frames:

The classification loss (ℒcls) uses cross-entropy to measure discrepancy between predicted and
ground-truth class labels, while the localization loss (ℒbox) combines L1 loss for coordinate
distances and generalized IoU loss for handling varying box sizes. Following DETR [10], 𝜎𝑡(𝑚)
denotes the index of the prediction matched to ground-truth object 𝑚, and 𝑀𝑡is the number of
objects in frame 𝑡. The hyperparameters 𝜆cls and 𝜆box control the weighting of the classification
and localization losses.
4.4 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS
4.4.1 DATASETS
In this work, we utilized the FSVOD-500, FSYTV-40, VidOR, and VidVRD datasets, as proposed
and repurposed in [110, 15]. These datasets are specifically designed for few-shot video object
detection, providing a structured evaluation framework with disjoint training, validation, and
testing classes. Detailed statistics of the datasets are provided in Table 4.1.
4.4.2 BASELINES
We evaluate our approach against temporal and non-temporal based few-shot object detection
methods. The selection of baselines aims to provide comprehensive comparisons across different
architectural paradigms and learning approaches. We exclude some results on certain datasets
due to the unavailability of public codebases, such as TACF [135] on VidVRD and VidOR.
45

Table 4.1: Overview of the datasets used in this work.
Non-temporal Methods. To establish strong non-temporal baselines, we compare against several
image-based few-shot detectors as discussed and compared in [110]. Traditional object detectors
like Faster R-CNN [8] serve as fundamental baselines to validate the benefits of few-shot learning
techniques. We include specialized few-shot detectors from [110] such as FSOD [117], RDN [139].
Further we included CoAE [11] designed for one-shot object detection and we extended it for
few-shot detection by finding the best confident detections. CoAE leverage meta-learning and
attention mechanisms to handle limited training data. Recent transformer-based approaches
like OWL-ViT [6] and UP-DETR [129] are included to evaluate the effectiveness of large-scale
pre-training. We also compare against a retrieval-based methods [140] that consider few-shot
detection as the retrieval from the pool of proposals.
Temporal Methods. For temporal modeling comparisons, we include several video-based
approaches. First, we incorporate methods adapted in [110], including MEGA [13], Ctracker
[141], FairMOT [142], and CenterTrack [134]. Second, we evaluate modern tracking-by-detection
approaches, DeepSort+YOLOv8 [143], TrackFormer [144], and ByteTrack [145] combined with
CLIP [50] to measure similarity between detected object tubes and support images. For these
approaches, we train models using examples from the support set to identify spatio-temporal
tubes, then apply average pooling to compute similarities with support images, selecting tubes
with maximum similarity across support images. Third, we compare against SoTA one/few-shot
video detectors, including FSVOD [110], TACF [135], and QDETRv [15], which provide direct
comparisons with our proposed method. Finally, we adapt Video Owl-ViT [7] by replacing its
text-encoder with an OWL-ViT image-encoder and incorporating our few-shot detection and
classification heads.
4.4.3 PERFORMANCE MEASURE
Following [110], we use Average Precision (AP) as evaluation metric. AP quantifies object
detection accuracy by measuring the area under the precision-recall curve. AP50 and AP75 are
variants that evaluate performance at specific Intersection over Union (IoU) thresholds of 50%
and 75%, respectively.
4.4.4 IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
We implement our model using a OWL ViT-L/16 for the vision encoder, pretrained with CLIP.
The temporal fusion module employs a 4-head cross-attention mechanism with 1024-dimensional
46


hidden states. The classification and localization heads use 2-layer MLPs with 512-dimensional
hidden states. During training, we use AdamW optimization with a 1e-5 learning rate, 0.01
weight decay, and cosine scheduling with a linear warmup. The loss weighting parameters
are set to 𝜆cls = 2, 𝜆box = 5. We set 𝜏= 0.94 and 𝜅= 0.98 in our experiments. Our model was
converged in four days using two Nvidia-A6000 GPUs.

4.4.5 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION
4.4.6 MAIN RESULTS
Our proposed approach achieves state-of-the-art performance across all four benchmark datasets.
On FSVOD-500, we achieve AP of 30.6%, AP50 of 42.9%, and AP75 of 32.1%, surpassing TACF
by margins of 3.7%, 3.7%, and 4.2%, highlighting robustness in localization and classification.
Similarly, on FSYTV-40, our method yields 21.2% AP, 29.8% AP50, and 23.5% AP75, outperforming
TACF by 5.3%, 5.6%, and 5.6%. Performance gains are more pronounced on VidOR and VidVRD,
49.4% AP, 57.3% AP50, and 50.2% AP75 for VidOR, and 48.7% AP, 57.8% AP50, and 49.2%
AP75 for VidVRD, setting new benchmarks. These results underscore the effectiveness of
our object-aware temporal modelling in capturing complex spatiotemporal dependencies with
minimal supervision.
4.4.7 TEMPORAL VS. NON-TEMPORAL METHODS
The performance comparison clearly demonstrates the advantage of temporal methods over
non-temporal.
While non-temporal methods like UP-DETR and FSOD show competitive
performance, they fundamentally fail to capture temporal dependencies crucial for video object
detection. This is evidenced by the consistent performance gap between across all datasets.

For instance, on FSVOD-500, even the best non-temporal method (UP-DETR with 20.1% AP)
falls significantly short compared to our approach (30.6% AP), representing a substantial 10.5%
48


absolute improvement. Similarly, on FSYTV-40, our method outperforms UP-DETR by 9.4%.
This highlight the critical importance of modeling temporal information in video object detection,
particularly in the few-shot set-up where limited examples make temporal continuity an essential
cue for accurate detection.
4.4.8 COMPARISON WITH ADAPTED TEMPORAL METHODS
Despite leveraging strong trackers (ByteTrack, DeepSort, TrackFormer) with CLIP’s rich semantic
representations, these methods consistently underperform compared to dedicated video object
detection architectures. The performance gap is substantial across all datasets: on FSVOD-500,
the best tracker-based approach (ByteTrack+CLIP) achieves only 14.7% AP, less than half of
our method’s 30.6% AP. Similar patterns emerge on other datasets, with gaps of 13.3% AP on
FSYTV-40, 24.5% AP on VidOR, and 23.1% AP on VidVRD. This significant under performance
reveals that tracking methods struggle with few-shot detection when adopted without explicit
few-shot modules.
4.4.9 IMPROVEMENTS OVER SPECIALIZED FEW-SHOT METHODS:
Our approach demonstrates significant improvements over previous specialized SoTA few-shot
video detection methods, which represent the most direct competitors to our work. Compared
to FSVOD, which pioneered dedicated architectures for few-shot video object detection, we
achieve consistent gains across all datasets: 5.5% AP on FSVOD-500, 6.6% AP on FSYTV-40, 4.3%
AP on VidOR, and 8.0% AP on VidVRD. Similar, observation we got for specialized few-shot
method, TACF. Similarly, our approach significantly outperformed the adapted Video OWL-ViT
method. The consistent performance gains over these specialized methods highlight several key
advantages of our approach: (1) more effective temporal aggregation through our object-aware
frame encoder, (2) better feature matching between support and query examples using few-shot
designed heads.
4.4.10 ADVANTAGE OF TEMPORAL FUSION (OBJECT AWARE DECODER)
In Table 4.4 on FSVOD-500, AP rises from 25.4% to 30.6% (+5.2%), AP50 from 37.1% to 42.9%
(+5.8%), and AP75 from 26.9% to 32.1% (5.2%) when temporal fusion used. On FSYTV-40, AP
improves from 19.2% to 21.2% (+2.0%), AP50 from 26.8% to 29.8% (+3.0%), and AP75 from 21.4%
to 23.5% (+2.1%). These gains highlight the decoder’s enhanced temporal reasoning detection
performance.
4.4.11 EFFECT OF THRESHOLD (𝜏) ON TEMPORAL PROPAGATION
Figure 4.4 demonstrates the relationship between confidence threshold and detection perfor-
mance. As the threshold decreases from 0.98 to 0.70, the average number of detections increases
from 2 to 23, indicating more objects are detected at lower confidence levels. The AP follows a
49

QDETRv
Video OWL-ViT
Ours
FSVOD
QDETRv
Video OWL-ViT
Ours
…
…

Figure 4.5: . Qualitative comparison of detection results across SoTA methods for two test videos from
FSVOD-500. Our approach accurately detects multiple instances of the Bactrian camel (top) having a
different visual appearance and precisely localizes the Autogyro (bottom), even as it moves farther from
the camera in the subsequent frames. Notably, competing methods miss some camel instances, while
Video OWL-ViT fails to detect all instances in the first two frames.
non-linear pattern, initially improving as the threshold decreases, reaching a peak of 30.6 at a
threshold of 0.94, before gradually declining to 17.7 at 0.70. This pattern suggests that while
lower thresholds increase detection quantity, they also introduce false positives that reduce
precision. The optimal threshold (0.94) represents the balance point between detection coverage
and precision, with performance degrading when the threshold is excessively relaxed. Since
the optimum is a property of the confidence distribution of the vision–language scores, and
that distribution shifts with object size, scene clutter and category granularity, the best value
of 𝜏is dataset-dependent to some degree. In practice the same value transfers across our four
benchmarks without per-dataset retuning, which suggests that the sensitivity is mild for datasets
of broadly similar composition, though a domain differing substantially in these respects would
warrant repeating this ablation. We did not explore an automatic mechanism for setting 𝜏.
The most natural option would replace the absolute threshold with a per-frame quantile of the
score distribution, propagating the top-𝑞% of detections, which self-calibrates when the score
distribution shifts and moves the tuning burden to 𝑞, a considerably less sensitive quantity. A
small learned gate over the detection embedding and its score, trained jointly with the detection
loss, would remove the hyper-parameter altogether at the cost of a more complex propagation
module.

4.4.12 FEW-SHOT PERFORMANCE COMPARISON
Table 4.3 shows consistent improvement of our method across baselines in 1-shot, 3-shot, and
10-shot settings. In the 1-shot case, we achieve 27.4% AP on FSVOD-500 and 18.3% on FSYTV-40,
outperforming FSVOD by 6.7% and 6.9%, respectively. In the 10-shot setup, our method attains
33.2% AP on FSVOD-500 and 23.5% on FSYTV-40, with gains of 6.0% and 7.4% over FSVOD.
Similar improvements across other benchmarks further validate our approach.
4.4.13 ERROR TYPE ANALYSIS
Following TIDE [146], we analyze error types in Fig. 4.6. Classification errors dominate all
approaches (19-28 AP@0.5), with our method achieving lowest classification (19) and localization
(5) errors. Video-OWLViT excels in background handling (0.7) but suffers from high classification
errors (28), while QDETRv performs the poorest overall. Improving feature discrimination and
localization precision are key challenges.
4.4.14 QUALITATIVE RESULTS
Figure 4.5 shows qualitative results on two videos from FSVOD-500. Our method demonstrates
several advantages. In the first video, our approach successfully detects multiple instances of the
same object category (bactrian camel), while competing methods miss some instances. Video
OWL-ViT fails to detect all instances in the first two frames, highlighting the effectiveness of
our object-aware decoder. In the second video featuring an autogiro, most methods can detect
the object, but our approach provides more accurate bounding boxes. QDETRv particularly
struggles when the object becomes smaller in the frame.

13.2
Table 4.5: Inference cost and accuracy on FSVOD-500. Our method is both faster and more accurate than
QDETRv and Video OWL-ViT; the only trade-off is against FSVOD, which is leaner but substantially less
accurate.
4.4.15 EFFICIENCY ANALYSIS.
Our method achieves a strong balance between accuracy and efficiency, processing at 14 FPS
with 8.1 GB of memory while attaining 30.6 AP. This is 40% faster than VideoOWL-ViT (10 FPS,
8.4 GB) and 27% faster than QDETRv (11 FPS, 7.6 GB), while providing substantially higher
accuracy (+4.8 AP and +4.5 AP, respectively). Although FSVOD operates at 16 FPS with lower
memory usage (5.8 GB), our approach delivers a +5.5% AP improvement with only a minor
trade-off. Compared to other baselines, DeepSort runs at 9 FPS (7.9 GB), CenterTrack at 12 FPS
(6.2 GB), and UP-DETR at 13 FPS (7.4 GB).
Table 4.5 collects these measurements. The comparison against QDETRv is worth stating
explicitly, since our framework builds on a heavier vision–language backbone and might be
expected to pay for it in latency. It does not: our method is 27% faster than QDETRv while
gaining 4.5 AP, for only 0.5 GB of additional memory. The reason is architectural. We dispense
with explicit object-tube proposals, which are the expensive component in tube-based methods,
and the filtering mechanism propagates only high-confidence object features, so the number of
tokens carried across frames remains small. The one genuine trade-off is against FSVOD, which
is faster (16 FPS) and leaner (5.8 GB) at 5.5 AP lower accuracy; for a memory-constrained edge
deployment where the difference between 5.8 GB and 8.1 GB is the binding constraint, FSVOD
remains defensible, whereas with an 8 GB budget available our method dominates on both axes.
4.4.16 COMPARISON AGAINST A GENERIC VIDEO-LLM
To test the claim that specialised grounding still outperforms generic vision–language models on
this task, we additionally evaluated Qwen3.5-VL [147] under the same protocol as our method,
supplying the support image as the visual exemplar and scoring the predicted boxes with
the same AP measure. The comparison is therefore like-for-like in the information available
to each model. Qwen3.5-VL [147] reaches 15.6 AP on FSVOD-500 and 8.3 AP on FSYTV-40,
well below our 30.6 and 21.2 respectively. We would not attribute this gap to model capacity.
Qwen3.5-VL [147] matches an exemplar within a frame but has no mechanism for maintaining
object identity across frames, so its detections are temporally inconsistent, which is precisely
what the feature-propagation component supplies.
52


4.5 SUMMARY
In this chapter, we introduced a novel approach for Few-Shot Video Object Detection that
incorporates a language-aligned vision encoder and a filtering mechanism to selectively propagate
high-confidence object features across frames. This mechanism efficiently enhances feature
progression, reduces noise accumulation, and improves detection accuracy in a few-shot video
setting. Through extensive experiments on four datasets, our approach consistently outperforms
state-of-the-art methods, achieving superior detection performance and demonstrating its
effectiveness in real-world video object detection tasks.
In the next chapter, we shift from grounding individual objects to grounding the interactions
between them, that is, understanding how two objects relate and behave together.
53


5
Few-Shot Relationships Grounding in
Videos
Interpreting visual relationships is a core aspect of comprehensive video understanding. Given
a query visual relationship as <subject, predicate, object> and a test video, our objective is to
localize the subject and object that are connected via the predicate. Given modern visio-lingual
understanding capabilities, solving this problem is achievable, provided that there are large-scale
annotated training examples available. However, annotating for every combination of subject,
object, and predicate is cumbersome, expensive, and possibly infeasible. Therefore, there
is a need for models that can learn to spatially and temporally localize subjects and objects
that are connected via an unseen predicate using only a few support set videos sharing the
common predicate. We address this challenging problem, referred to as few-shot referring
relationships in videos for the first time. To this end, we pose the problem as a minimization of
an objective function defined over a T-partite random field. Here, the vertices of the random
field correspond to candidate bounding boxes for the subject and object, and T represents the
number of frames in the test video. This objective function is composed of frame-level and visual
relationship similarity potentials. To learn these potentials, we use a relation network that takes
query-conditioned translational relationship embedding as inputs and is meta-trained using
support set videos in an episodic manner. Further, the objective function is minimized using
a belief propagation-based message passing on the random field to obtain the spatiotemporal
localization or subject and object trajectories. We perform extensive experiments using two
public benchmarks, namely ImageNet-VidVRD and VidOR, and compare the proposed approach
with competitive baselines to assess its efficacy.

Figure 5.1: The proposed problem setup. (a). Given a query visual relationship as <subject, predicate,
object> and a test video, our goal is to localize the subject and object on the test video with the help
of a support set containing a few videos with the same predicate. In this example, the goal is to
spatio-temporally localize the plane (subject) and person (object) that are connected via fly above (predicate),
with the help of a support set containing only four videos sharing predicate fly above. It should be noted
here that fly above is unseen during training. We refer this problem as few-shot referring relationship in
videos. This problem setup is inspired by the real-world scenario where obtaining large-scale annotations
for every visual relationship is practically infeasible. As shown in (b), a popular visual relationship
video dataset, namely ImageNet-VidVRD, contains many predicates with very few examples, i.e., it has a
long-tail distribution. Further, as shown in (c), the success of a modern visual relationship localization
technique (vRGV) [4] is clearly proportional to predicate distribution in the training set. This calls a need
for solving referring relationship tasks in a few-shot setup. We propose this task and present a novel
principled solution.
The subsequent sections present the introduction, a review of related work, the proposed
methodology, and a detailed analysis of results and discussions.
5.1 INTRODUCTION
Consider the following problem: given a video, a visual relationship query represented as a
<subject, predicate, object> tuple, and a support set of a few videos containing the same predicate
but not necessarily the same subjects and objects, our objective is to spatially and temporally
localize both subjects and objects that are related via the predicate within the video. We refer to
this problem as Few-shot Referring Relationship and illustrate it in Figure 5.1. Solving this problem
has the potential to benefit cross-task video understanding [148] and video retrieval [149, 150],
among other applications. Identifying its utility, referring relationship task for images has
been first introduced by [18]. However, referring relationships in videos poses additional
video-specific challenges, such as understanding dynamic visual relationships. Some of these
challenges have been addressed in recent research by Xiao et al. [4], but with a reliance on strong
supervision. Referring relationships in videos within a few-shot setup is an under-explored area.
We aim to fill this research gap via our work.
Visual relationships inherently have long-tail distributions in any video collection. For
example, Image-Net VidVRD [114] dataset includes approximately 18.9% predicates with more
than 100 instances but 20.5% predicates with less than 10 instances. This phenomenon is also
shown in Figure 5.1, where most predicates belong to the tail side of the distribution. The
methods that work best for frequent visual relationships do not necessarily generalize well to
unseen visual relationships. Moreover, in a real-world scenario annotating visual relationships
for each combination of subject, object, and predicate are cumbersome, expensive, and possibly
infeasible. Therefore, there is a need to study visual relationship tasks in a few-shot setup. For
instance: only with a few examples of the fly above predicate, such as videos containing <bird, fly
above, person>, <helicopter, fly above, train> as shown in Figure 5.1 (a), a model should be able to
generalize to the unseen visual relationship, such as <plane, fly above, person>. We propose a
solution for Few-shot Referring Relationship in videos in this work.
We pose the problem of a few-shot referring relationship in the video as a minimization of
an objective function defined over a 𝑇-partite random field where 𝑇is the number of frames in
the test video. Furthermore, the vertices of the random field are treated as random variables and
55


represent candidate bounding boxes for the subject and objects. The objective function consists
of frame-level potentials and visual relationship similarity potentials, both of which are learned
using a relation network that takes query-conditioned translational relationship embeddings
as inputs. We meta-train the relation network using support set videos in an episodic manner.
Further, the objective function is minimized using a belief propagation-based message passing
on the random field to obtain subject and object trajectories. We perform extensive experiments
on two public benchmarks, namely ImageNet-VidVRD [114] and VidOR [151], and report the
accuracy of localizing subject, object, and relation, denoted by 𝐴𝑠𝑢𝑏, 𝐴𝑜𝑏𝑗, and 𝐴𝑟, respectively,
along with other popular measures used in the literature. Our proposed approach clearly
outperforms the related baselines.
The contributions of this chapter are three folds. (i) We propose a novel problem setup for
referring relationship in videos, where the model must learn to localize the subject and object
corresponding to a query visual relationship that was unseen during training using only a few
support videos. (ii) We propose a new formulation to solve this task based on the minimization
of an objective function on 𝑇-partite random field where 𝑇is the number of frames in the test
video, and the vertices of the random field representing potential bounding boxes for subject
and objects correspond to the random variables. (Section 5.3.1). (iii) Additionally, to enrich
query-conditioned relational embeddings, we present two aggregation techniques, namely
global semantic and local localization aggregations. The use of these aggregation techniques
results in enhanced relationship representations, which helps to obtain better trajectories for
objects and subjects related via the query visual relationship. This is evidenced by extensive
experiments and ablations. (Sections 5.3.2 and 5.4.5).
5.2 RELATED WORK
Visual Relationships: Interpreting visual relationships in images [152, 16, 153] as well as
videos [55, 154, 155, 156, 157] have gained huge attention over the last few years. They have also
been key components of large-scale popular datasets such as Visual Genome [158] and Action
Genome [5]. Visual relationships were studied with respect to object segmentation to leverage
spatial relations [159] and to understand human-object interactions [160] via human-centric
relationships. Krishna et al. [158] proposed the concept of scene graphs by combining multiple
visual relationships in a graph structure. The structured representation of the scene graph
is exploited for several tasks, including image retrieval [161]. Shang et al. [114] extended
scene graphs from images to videos. Videos have spatiotemporal nature introducing dynamic
relations that are not present in images. Scene graphs in videos represent fine-grain information
that helps in the downstream task for spatiotemporal reasoning. Several works have been
introduced that utilize visual relationships to improve downstream tasks such as an image
or video captioning [17], visual grounding [162], visual question answering [163]. To localize
subjects and objects connected via a query visual relationship, Krishna et al. [18] proposed
referring relationship for images. Their proposed method used an iterative message-passing
mechanism between subject and object using language priors to ground the query relations.
Further, Xiao et al. [4] extended referring relationship to videos and refer it as visual relationship
grounding in videos. In a similar spirit, but, in a few-shot set-up, we propose to spatiotemporally
localize subjects and objects in a video for a given relationship.

Few-Shot Learning: Few-shot learning in the literature can be grouped into: (i) metric-based
[77, 76, 80] and (ii) model-based [81, 164, 78] methods. We limit our discussion to only closely
related works. Vinyals et al. [76] proposed matching networks that learn to compare using a
small support set. Further, Sung et al. [78] proposed a relation network that learns from a few
labeled images of the support set by comparing the query image. In this work, we have used a
relation network to learn relationship similarity using the given support set videos, and this
enabled us to obtain both frame-level and visual relationship similarity potentials.
5.3 PROPOSED METHOD
Task Definition: Given an unseen visual relationship consisting of subject (𝑠), predicate (𝑝), and
object (𝑜), i.e. 𝑟= <𝑠, 𝑝, 𝑜> as a query and a test video 𝑣along with a small support set of
𝐾videos 𝑆𝑠𝑢𝑝= {<𝑠𝑖, 𝑝, 𝑜𝑖>, 𝑣𝑖}𝐾
𝑖=1 containing the same predicate 𝑝, the goal is to obtain the
sequence of bounding boxes (also known as trajectories) 𝑇∗
𝑠and 𝑇∗
𝑜corresponding to subject and
object, respectively on the test video 𝑣. We refer to this task as few-shot referring relationship in
videos. As an example, in Figure 5.1 (a), 𝑟= <plane, fly above, person> is a query that needs to be
spatiotemporally localized on a test video using a support set that contains four videos of fly
above predicate. We pose this task as an optimization problem on a 𝑇-partite random field which
we describe next.
57


5.3.1 FEW-SHOT REFERRING RELATIONSHIP AS AN OPTIMIZATION ON A 𝑇-PARTITE RANDOM
FIELD
Given a test video 𝑣, we split it into 𝑇frames. Then, we obtain 𝑀most confident object bounding
boxes on each frame using FasterRCNN [8]. Video 𝑣can be represented as a sequence of
extracted bounding boxes, 𝑣= {𝐵𝑗
𝑖|𝑖∈[1, 𝑇], 𝑗∈[1, 𝑀]}. The pair of these bounding boxes in
each frame is a candidate solution for referring relationship task. While finding the optimal
solution using a brute force technique is combinatorial and practically infeasible, we solve it
using optimization on a 𝑇-partite random field. To this end, we construct a 𝑇-partite graph
𝐺= (𝒱, ℰ) representing the test video as follows: for a frame 𝑖, we represent each pair of 𝑀
bounding boxes as nodes and their all possible next and previous-frame connections as edges.
More precisely, the set of vertices 𝒱for a video 𝑣contains ordered pair of bounding boxes
as follows: {𝑢𝑗𝑘
𝑖
= (𝐵𝑗
𝑖, 𝐵𝑘
𝑖) : 𝑖, ∈[1, 𝑇]; 𝑗, 𝑘∈[1, 𝑀]; 𝑗≠𝑘}, and the set of edges is defined as
ℰ= {(𝑢𝑗,𝑘

random variable that takes one of two labels {select (1), reject (0)} for the given query 𝑟. Figure 5.2
illustrates the construction of graph 𝐺for a three-frame test video. It is worth making the cost of
this construction explicit, since the term “𝑇-partite random field” may suggest a more expensive
inference procedure than is actually required. Each frame contributes 𝑀(𝑀−1) binary nodes,
and the edge set defined above connects only the same ordered pair (𝑗, 𝑘) across consecutive
frames. The graph therefore decomposes into 𝑀(𝑀−1) independent chains of length 𝑇. Belief
propagation on a chain is exact and linear in its length, so inference costs 𝑂(𝑇· 𝑀2) over binary
variables and is trivially parallel across the chains. The cost is thus linear in the number of
frames, which means that long videos are not the binding constraint; the quadratic term is
𝑀, so doubling the number of candidate boxes per frame quadruples the node count, and
densely annotated settings are the harder case. Where a video is long enough for memory to
become a concern, the natural mitigations are sampling keyframes, since a relationship persists
across adjacent frames and not every frame need form a graph layer, and capping 𝑀through
proposal suppression. It should be noted here that the selected nodes (corresponding subject
and object bounding box) from each frame form subject and object trajectories. To obtain optimal

(ii) Local Localization Aggregation (LLA): For adding the context from the adjacent
frames as a local localization context, we considered a window size of five, i.e., for 𝑖𝑡ℎframe,

5.3.3 LEARNING RELATION NETWORK WITH FEW EXAMPLES
The proposed problem formulation has to learn the similarity between object pairs as the visual
relationships using a few videos from 𝑆𝑠𝑢𝑝. We have selected the Relation Network (𝑅𝜽) [78], a
metric-based meta-learning approach, as our method of choice. After learning the similarity
measure for the unseen predicate, 𝑅𝜽is used to compute both frame-level as well as visual
relationship similarity potentials.
For a pair of object pairs 𝑝1 = (𝑥1, 𝑦1) and 𝑝2 = (𝑥2, 𝑦2), their representation as a visual
relationship, 𝑓𝑝1

For each support set video, we extract ground truth positive and negative object pairs.
