5.3.4 TRAJECTORY GENERATION
To generate the final trajectories of the subject and object, we used three different optimization
approaches. In the first technique, we used only frame-level potential Ψ𝑖to select a node with
the optimal potential value, which was determined as the minimum value among all the nodes
at frame 𝑖. We skipped a frame if the optimal potential value is higher than the fixed threshold
(= −0.5). As an alternative to this, we also used a greedy solver by selecting a node from frame 𝑖
using the first technique, and then, we find the next node in the next frame 𝑗with optimal Ψ𝑖𝑗.
We skipped a frame 𝑗if the optimal Ψ𝑖𝑗potential value is higher than the fixed threshold (= −0.5).
In our full model, we used both frame-level potential and visual relationship similarity potential
and used belief propagation using message passing [167] to find the optimal trajectories.
5.4 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS
5.4.1 DATASETS
We have used two video benchmark datasets, namely, VidOR [113, 151] and ImageNet-
VidVRD [114] in our experiments. ImageNet-VidVRD contains 1000 videos obtained from
the ILVSRC2016-VID dataset [130]. It has 132 predicates and 35 object categories. VidOR is
a large-scale dataset containing 10,000 videos obtained from YFCC100M collection. VidOR
contains 50 predicates and 80 object categories. For our problem setting, we split both datasets
62


into disjoint sets based on predicates and videos by randomly assigning 35 and 15 predicates to
the train and test sets of VidOR, while assigning 88 and 22 predicates to the train and test sets of
ImageNet-VidVRD, respectively.
5.4.2 PERFORMANCE MEASURE
In this work, we adopt widely-used evaluation metrics in the referring relationship and video
understanding literature, as described in [18, 168, 4]. Specifically, we calculate the subject and
object accuracy, which represents the percentage of correct trajectories returned by the model
for the entire test set. A relation accuracy is defined as the percentage of correct subject and
object pair trajectories. Here, a pair is considered correct if both subject and object trajectories
are correct. For spatiotemporal accuracy, 𝐴𝑠𝑢𝑏
𝑠−𝑡, 𝐴𝑜𝑏𝑗
𝑠−𝑡, of the subject or object, a trajectory is
considered correct if at least 50% of the bounding boxes across frames have ≥0.5 intersection
over union (IoU) with respect to ground truth bounding boxes. Similarly, for spatiotemporal
accuracy of relation, 𝐴𝑟
𝑠−𝑡, a pair of subject and object is considered true only if both trajectories
are spatiotemporally accurate (i.e. at least 50% of the bounding boxes across frames have ≥0.5
IoU). For spatial accuracy, 𝐴𝑠𝑢𝑏
𝑠
, 𝐴𝑜𝑏𝑗
𝑜, a trajectory is considered true if the average of IoUs of the
bounding box with respect to ground truth is at least 0.5. The mean IoU for a trajectory is an
average of the IoU score for all bounding boxes with respect to ground truth. The mean IoU of
the subject or object, 𝑚𝐼𝑜𝑈𝑠𝑢𝑏
𝑠
, 𝑚𝐼𝑜𝑈𝑜𝑏𝑗
𝑠
is the average of the mean IoU of the subject or object
for the entire test set.
5.4.3 BASELINES
To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to approach referring relationships in videos in a
few-shot setup, and there are no existing baselines in the literature that can be directly compared
to our approach. Therefore, we adopt closely related methods as our baselines to compare the
effectiveness of our method as follows:
(I) FEW-SHOT VISUAL RELATION CO-LOCALIZATION (VRC):
Few-shot VRC has originally been proposed for localizing common subjects and objects in a bag
of images in a few-shot setup [169]. We adopt it for videos by treating object trajectory pairs in
the test and support set videos as a bag and performing visual relationship co-localization at the
frame level.
(II) VISUAL VRGV [4]
tackles the same task as ours but differs significantly in supervision. We adapted vRGV for our
few-shot problem setup for a fair comparison. Recall that in our problem setup, the model is
trained in episodes, and each episode contains a support set of a few videos and a test video for
evaluation. We utilize all available videos from the train set to train the vRGV model. During
63


testing for each episode, we fine-tune the model on the support set and then perform localization
for the given query visual relationship on the test video. We refer to these baselines as vRGV in
a few-shot setup or vRGV-fs.
(III) TRACKLET-BASED APPROACH:
In this section, we provide a detailed explanation of the proposed tracklet-based. Consider
notations independent of the main method.
Tracklet Detector. For each video, using a pre-trained detector, we extract all tracklets (repre-
sented as a set 𝑇𝑒) features and spatio-temporal locations. Each entity 𝑒𝑖∈𝑇𝑒having length 𝑙𝑖as
number of frames is categorized by a time slot (𝑠𝑒
𝑖, 𝑒𝑒
𝑖) and bounding box coordinate 𝑏𝑖∈𝑅𝑙𝑖×4. For
each entity 𝑒𝑖, tracklet feature 𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑖is a combination of spatial and appearance features. Spatial
feature 𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑠
𝑖∈𝑅𝑙𝑖×8 is formed as the concatenation of all box offset Δ𝑏𝑖and coordinate 𝑏𝑖, where
Δ𝑏𝑖,𝑗is defined between two consecutive frames as box coordinate offset, i.e., Δ𝑏𝑖,𝑗= 𝑏𝑖,𝑗+1 −𝑏𝑖,𝑗.
For appearance 𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑎
𝑖∈𝑅𝑙𝑖×𝑑𝑎each frame is extracted using RoIAlign [8] based on box locations.
For entity 𝑒𝑖, the tracklet feature 𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑖∈𝑅𝑙𝑖×𝑑𝑒is formed as,
𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑖= 𝑓𝑎[ 𝑓𝑏( 𝑓𝑒𝑎𝑡𝑎
𝑖); 𝑓𝑐( 𝑓𝑠

Metric Learner. This module aims to rank the pair proposals produced by the pair proposal
module. A deep metric is learned with the help of support set videos in a few-shot way. Deep
metric learns to project positive pair proposals (pair proposals with the same predicate) close in
the feature space while negative pair proposals (pair proposals with different predicates) are far
away.

Training and Inference. The top-ranked feature contains the tracklet information of the
subject and object related to the query predicate. But the query visual relation might not span
the whole duration of the subject and object and can be in multiple time slots. To correctly find
the duration and probable multiple slots we learn another linear model to predict each frame
whether it belongs to a query relationship or not. The linear model is defined as,
𝑟𝑑= 𝑠𝑜𝑓𝑡𝑚𝑎𝑥(𝑀𝐿𝑃([ 𝑓𝑒𝑚𝑏; 𝑓𝑡]))
(5.26)

of subject and object, we have a score for starting of the relationship and similarly for the end of
a relationship. This module is trained using the following supervised loss,
ℒ3 = −𝑡𝐼𝑜𝑈(𝑏𝑏𝑜𝑥𝑔𝑡, 𝑏𝑏𝑜𝑥𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑑)
(5.27)

the ground truth bounding boxes. The start and end of the query relation are extracted as,
𝐹𝑠= {𝑖; 𝑟𝑑[𝑖] > 𝜖}𝑑/2
𝑖=1, 𝐹𝑒= {𝑖; 𝑟𝑑[𝑖] > 𝜖}𝑑
𝑖=𝑑/2
(5.28)
The frames which are not contained within the common duration of the subject and object
are removed. The lowest frame number 𝑖𝑠is selected from 𝐹𝑠as the start of the relationship and
the lowest frame from 𝑖𝑒such that 𝑖𝑒> 𝑖𝑠is selected from 𝐹𝑒as the end of the relation. The
frames contained in between 𝑖𝑠and 𝑖𝑒are removed from 𝐹𝑠and 𝐹𝑒to find another query relation
instant in a similar way.
Finally, all modules are combined during training as an end-to-end differential function. The
overall model is trained using the following loss function,

5.4.4 IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
For frame-level detection, we utilized FasterRCNN [8], which was pre-trained on MS-COCO
with ResNet-101 as the backbone. We extracted the 30 most confident object bounding boxes for
each frame. Our implementation was done using PyTorch. We optimized the model parameters
using Adam [131] with an initial learning rate of 1e-5, while also employing a dropout rate of 0.3
to reduce overfitting. To prevent overfitting, we used early stopping. We trained the model on
an Nvidia-RTX A6000 GPU.

5.4.5 RESULTS AND DISCUSSIONS
We compare our approach with baselines discussed in Section 5.4.3 on VidOR and ImageNet-
VidVRD datasets in Table 5.2. We observe that the baselines modified using prior works, namely
VRC and vRGV, exhibit weaker performance across all the evaluated metrics. The tracklet-based
approach performs marginally better than these baselines. However, its performance is heavily
bottle-necked with the quality of tracklets generated. Our approach, without the proposed global
semantic and local localization aggregation itself, surpasses these baselines. We also observed
a positive impact of frame-level and visual relationship similarity potential; for example, in
Table 5.2, the accuracy of localizing relationship (𝐴𝑟
𝑠−𝑡) has increased by 2.3% (absolute scale) in
the full model with respect to the greedy solver on ImageNet-VidVRD dataset. The superior
performance of our method can be attributed to the principled optimization framework, the
proposed aggregation techniques, query-conditioned learning of relationship embeddings, and
the effective utilization of support set videos to meta-train relation networks. To show the
efficacy of our method and to justify the choice of different modules, we conduct the following
ablations and analyses:

GLOBAL SEMANTIC AND LOCAL LOCALIZATION AGGREGATION:
We remove global semantic (GSA) and local localization aggregation (LLA) in our full model

and object representation and analyze its effect on the ImageNet-VidVRD dataset in Table 5.3.
We observe that our full model that uses these two aggregation techniques performs better as
compared to the settings where one or both of these are removed.

In the second ablation, we justify the choice of translation visual relationship embedding. To
this end, we replaced the translational embedding in eq. (5.15) with a simple concatenation of
subject and object representation. As shown in Table 5.5, translational relationship embedding
used in our model is significantly more robust than simple concatenation embeddings.

performs reasonably well for localizing subjects and objects in videos, even in these cases with
an intuitive drop in performance as compared to cases where only the predicate is unseen, and
both subject and object are seen.
PERFORMANCE ON STATIC AND DYNAMIC RELATIONS:
In order to analyze the performance of our approach for different types of relations, we
present the predicate-wise results in Table 5.4. The results reveal that our proposed approach
generally achieves better performance for static relationships. Nonetheless, despite the additional
challenges posed by dynamic relationships, such as the presence of moving objects and subjects,
occlusion, motion blur, and deformation, our approach demonstrates reasonable results for
dynamic predicates highlighting its robustness. This gap follows directly from the structure
of the objective. The frame-level potential Ψ𝑖is computed from a single frame’s box pair and
therefore carries no motion information, while only the pairwise potential Ψ𝑖𝑗links consecutive
frames. Static predicates such as “beneath” and “above” are consequently decidable from Ψ𝑖
alone, whereas dynamic predicates such as “chase” and “follow” depend on Ψ𝑖𝑗, which is the

indicating the effectiveness of our approach.
5.5 SUMMARY
In this chapter, we approached the problem from frame-level object detection to video-level
trajectory generation by optimizing an objective function on a random field in a few-shot way. We
used global semantic and local localization aggregation to enhance query-conditioned translation
visual relationship embedding. The objective function is minimized using belief propagation
on the random field. We performed experimental comparisons along with ablation studies to
show the efficacy of our approach. We firmly believe that our work will open up several future
research towards the larger exciting goal of comprehensive cross-task video understanding.
70


In the next chapter, we move from spatial reasoning to temporal reasoning and focus on
identifying when key actions or moments occur within a video.
71


6
Moment Grounding in Videos
Video-to-video moment retrieval (Vid2VidMR) is the task of localizing unseen events or moments
in a target video using a query video. This task poses several challenges, such as the need
for semantic frame-level alignment and modeling complex dependencies between query and
target videos. To tackle this challenging problem, we introduce MATR (Moment Alignment
TRansformer), a transformer-based model designed to capture semantic context as well as the
temporal details necessary for precise moment localization. MATR conditions target video
representations on query video features using dual-stage sequence alignment that encodes
the required correlations and dependencies. These representations are then used to guide
foreground/background classification and boundary prediction heads, enabling the model
to accurately identify moments in the target video that semantically match with the query
video. Additionally, to provide a strong task-specific initialization for MATR, we propose a
self-supervised pre-training technique that involves training the model to localize random clips
within videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MATR achieves notable performance
improvements of 15.4% in R@1 and 11.4% in mIoU on an absolute scale compared to the
state-of-the-art methods on the popular ActivityNet-VRL dataset. Additionally, on our newly
proposed dataset, SportsMoments, MATR shows a 16.4% gain in R@1 and a 16.3% gain in mIoU
on an absolute scale over strong baselines.
The subsequent sections present the introduction, a review of related work, the proposed
methodology, and a detailed analysis of results and discussions.
6.1 INTRODUCTION
Video moment retrieval is the task of temporally localizing the start and end times of a moment1
in a target video described by a given query. Although text-based video moment retrieval has
been extensively explored [20, 21, 171, 24, 172], it often poses challenges for users attempting to
describe specific moments verbally. For example, consider a spectacular bicycle kick in soccer
(Fig. 6.1 top). Although an expert can search by naming or describing it in detail, a beginner in
soccer may struggle to describe this impressive move accurately. They might say something
like “The player kicked the ball while in the air", which lacks the nuance needed to convey
the athleticism and artistry involved. Such a description may lead to poor results for someone
searching for similar moments. In contrast, if a soccer trainee shows a short video clip of a bicycle
kick, the input is rich and crisp, making it easier for a retriever to locate similar moments in a
1A moment is a continuous segment of frames within a target video that best represents the actions, events, or
interactions described by the query.
72


“A stock man is lifting dumbbells to his sides with 
controlled motion to target his shoulder muscles, 
maintaining proper form throughout."
"A man positions himself beneath the ball, 
leaping into the air, bending his knees and 
arching his back. As he ﬂips backward, he 
connects with the ball using his foot."
Vid2Vid Moment Retrieval
Frame to 
Frame 
Alignment
Output Segment
Query Video
Target Video
Text Query
Video Query
Figure 6.1: (Top): Activity where text falls short to explain complex action or events, such as dumbbell side
raises and bicycle kick, necessitating the need for a more intuitive query modality for localizing semantically
matching moments. (Bottom): Shows the Vid2Vid moment retrieval setting, which is the goal of this
work.
target soccer match video. This approach aligns with how users naturally would prefer to search
content within the same (video) modality, making it a more effective method for retrieving
specific moments. Fig. 6.1 (bottom) shows a video cover drive query in cricket that is localized in
a target video.
Therefore, in this chapter, we study video-to-video moment retrieval (Vid2VidMR) where the
aim is to temporally localize a moment in the target video with a high semantic match between
the moment and the query video. Vid2VidMR, a task formally introduced by Feng et al. [173], has
many potential application areas, such as sports video analytics, educational content creation and
e-learning, and surveillance systems. This task is challenging and requires semantic frame-level
alignment and modeling complex dependencies between query and target videos. This task
is further complicated by the immense search space of thousands of frames, making efficient
yet accurate processing essential. The need for a semantic understanding of video content
and variety in video length, context, and action speed calls for adaptive models capable of
generalizing across diverse scenarios. Addressing these challenges demands advanced temporal
video modeling techniques.
To address the aforementioned challenges, we propose MATR (Moment Alignment
TRansformer) – a method that uses explicit ‘dual-stage sequence alignment’ to capture the
required correlation and temporal details essential for accurate moment localization.
By
conditioning target video representations on query features, MATR produces query-aligned
representations that encode the necessary correlations and temporal dependencies between
the two videos. We use differentiable dynamic time warping [174, 175] loss for aligning the
73


query and target videos, and represent the target video by conditioning it on the query video
to focus on correlated temporal features. These representations guide a classification head to
discriminate relevant moments from the irrelevant background and a boundary prediction head
to mark the start and end of the identified moment in the target video. Further, to enhance our
model’s generalization using unlabeled videos, we introduce a self-supervised pre-training
strategy which involves training MATR to localize randomly sampled clips within the same
video, enabling it to learn the moment localization skill in a self-supervised manner.
We evaluate MATR on public ActivityNet-VRL [173] benchmark and on our newly introduced
dataset on the sports domain, viz. SportsMoments, covering two of the most popular sports,
namely soccer and cricket. The proposed dataset SportsMoments contains complex video
queries that are hard to describe in text, as shown in Fig. 6.1. Also, SportsMoments provides a
challenging large-scale benchmark to provide a comprehensive study on vid-to-vid moment
retrieval task. Our approach achieves significant performance gains, with an improvement
of 15.4% and 11.4% in R@1 and mIoU, respectively, on ActivityNet-VRL outperforming the
state-of-the-art methods. Furthermore, MATR outperforms the implemented strong baselines
on our proposed data set with 16.4% gains in R@1 and 16.3% gains in mIoU, all on an absolute
scale.
Our contributions are as follows: (i) We introduce MATR, which uses explicit dual-stage
sequence alignment within a transformer framework between target and query video to capture
temporal correlations and dependencies for accurate moment localization. (ii) We propose a
self-supervised pre-training objective that enhances model initialization by understanding rich
video structure without requiring any labeled data. (iii) We conduct extensive experiments and
ablations to study the efficacy of our framework against competitive baselines and state-of-the-art
methods. Our findings offer valuable insights into our design choices, and our approach
advances the state-of-the-art on Vid2VidMR.
6.2 RELATED WORK
6.2.1 VIDEO MOMENT RETRIEVAL (VMR)
VMR has recently gained significant interest in the research community [176, 177, 178, 20, 21,
24, 172, 171, 179, 180, 181, 182, 183]. Unlike video action understanding tasks such as action
classification [184, 185, 186, 187, 165, 188, 189, 190] or temporal action localization [191, 192, 193],
VMR focuses on identifying segments that semantically align with a broader range of queries,
which may describe complex and context-specific moments. This focus on semantic alignment
rather than predefined action categories enables VMR to address more flexible and nuanced
video understanding scenarios, ranging from identifying specific events to capturing abstract
concepts in videos. The complexity of VMR stems from the need to understand both the query’s
semantics and the temporal dynamics within target videos, requiring sophisticated cross-modal
reasoning capabilities. Based on the query modality, existing VMR methods can be broadly
grouped into (i) textual query-based approaches like Moment-DETR [20], QD-DETR [21], Uni-
VTG [24], and (ii) video query-based approaches like GDP [23], FFI+SRM [22], SRL [25]. While
text-based VMR has seen extensive research attention due to the relative simplicity of handling
74


textual queries, video query-based approaches remain less explored despite their practical
importance in applications like video surveillance, sports analytics, and content recommendation
systems. Our work falls under video query-based VMR (Vid2VidMR). However, in addition to
developing a new approach tailored for video query, we also adapt several text-query-based
methods to make them suitable for Vid2VidMR and compare our method against both text and
video query-based methods.
6.2.2 ALIGNMENT IN VIDEOS
Alignment has been a thrust area in the video understanding community. It has been applied to
a wide range of video understanding tasks, including video retrieval [194], procedural steps
alignment [175], action recognition [195], anomaly detection [196], movie understanding [197],
and video synchronization [198, 199]. These alignment approaches vary in their formulation,
ranging from classical dynamic programming methods like Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) to
more recent neural network-based techniques that learn alignment patterns from data. The
effectiveness of alignment methods is particularly evident in tasks requiring the establishment
of temporal correspondence between different sequences or modalities. Researchers have also
explored sequence alignment for the text-VMR task, e.g., Chowdhury et al. [200] used sequence
alignment between CNN representation of frames and GRU representation of text query to
perform text-VMR. Their approach demonstrated that even simple alignment techniques could
improve localization performance by establishing correspondences between textual descriptions
and visual content. Jung et al. [201] used alignment to enhance semantic understanding between
query and target video at the abstract level for the text-VMR task. However, their alignment is
not at the sequence level. Despite these advances, most existing works utilize alignment in a
single stage of their pipelines, limiting their ability to capture both low-level visual similarities
and high-level semantic relationships simultaneously. Compared to the existing literature,
MATR goes beyond traditional sequence alignment by introducing a dual-stage alignment
mechanism, leveraging transformer-based feature fusion, and maintaining flexibility with
multiple alignment strategies.
These innovations enable it to achieve more accurate and
context-aware video moment retrieval compared to existing approaches.
6.2.3 VIDEO ENCODER PRE-TRAINING
Recent works [36, 202] have shown that pre-training on web-scale datasets can be highly
effective for various downstream tasks in natural language processing as well as computer
vision. The ability to transfer knowledge from large-scale datasets to specific tasks has become a
cornerstone of modern deep learning approaches. The video representation learning community
has also leveraged self-supervised pre-training techniques designed for training effective
encoders [203, 204, 205, 15, 206, 207, 129], which can be finetuned for enhanced performance
on downstream tasks. Unlike image-based pre-training, video pre-training must capture both
spatial and temporal dynamics, making it particularly challenging yet rewarding when successful.
These approaches have exploited temporal coherence and visual consistency in videos to learn
meaningful features. For example, studies like [129, 15] explored the random spatio-temporal
trajectory detection (constructed with a sequence of random patches) for object detection.
75


Inspired by these methods, which harness task-specific pre-training objectives for various video
analytics tasks, we propose a novel self-supervised pre-training objective specially designed for
the video-to-video moment retrieval task.
6.3 BACKGROUND: DYNAMIC TIME WARPING
Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) [208] is a widely used method for aligning and comparing
two sequences, such as time series, that may vary in length or exhibit local accelerations and
decelerations. Unlike the Euclidean distance, which requires sequences to be of the same length
and aligned in time, DTW is robust to temporal shifts and distortions, making it a popular choice
in speech, gesture, and time-series analysis [209].
Let X = [x1, . . . , x𝑚] ∈R𝑚×𝑑and Y = [y1, . . . , y𝑛] ∈R𝑛×𝑑denote two sequences of possibly
different lengths 𝑚and 𝑛, where 𝑑is the feature dimension. The pairwise dissimilarity between
sequence elements is encoded in a cost matrix C ∈R𝑚×𝑛, with entries 𝐶𝑖,𝑗= 𝛿(x𝑖, y𝑗) for a chosen
distance function 𝛿(e.g., squared Euclidean).
An alignment between X and Y is represented by a binary matrix A ∈{0, 1}𝑚×𝑛, where
A𝑖,𝑗= 1 indicates that x𝑖is matched to y𝑗. The set of all valid alignments, 𝒜𝑚,𝑛, enforces
monotonicity and continuity constraints so that alignments are contiguous and order-preserving.
DTW computes the minimum total alignment cost over all valid alignments:
DTW(X, Y) = min
A∈𝒜𝑚,𝑛
⟨A, C⟩,
where ⟨A, C⟩= Í𝑚
𝑖=1
Í𝑛
𝑗=1 A𝑖,𝑗𝐶𝑖,𝑗is the Frobenius inner product. This optimization can be
efficiently solved by dynamic programming in 𝒪(𝑚𝑛) time and space.
Limitations
DTW’s flexibility enables robust sequence comparison and alignment, even when
sequences are stretched or compressed in time. However, a key limitation of DTW is that it
is not differentiable with respect to its inputs, as the minimum over alignments introduces
discontinuities. This non-differentiability makes DTW unsuitable as a loss function in gradient-
based learning frameworks.
Soft-DTW Differentiable Relaxation
To address this, soft-DTW [209] replaces the hard
minimum in DTW with a differentiable soft-minimum (log-sum-exp) operator, parameterized
by a smoothing parameter 𝛾> 0. The soft-minimum operator is defined as:
min𝛾{𝑎1, . . . , 𝑎𝐾} = −𝛾log
𝐾
Õ
𝑘=1
exp

−𝑎𝑘
𝛾

.
The soft-DTW cost is then given by:
soft-DTW𝛾(X, Y) = min𝛾{⟨A, C⟩: A ∈𝒜𝑚,𝑛} .
76


As 𝛾→0, soft-DTW recovers the original DTW; for 𝛾> 0, it aggregates the costs of all possible
alignments, weighting them exponentially by their cost. This smoothing ensures that soft-DTW
is differentiable everywhere with respect to its inputs, enabling its use as a loss function for
end-to-end training of models that output sequences.
6.4 THE MATR MODEL
Our objective is to temporally localize a moment in a target video 𝑉𝑡using a query video
𝑉𝑞. We represent the target and query videos as sequences of 𝑀and 𝑁uniformly sampled
frames, respectively. We refer to this problem as Vid2VidMR. In this work, we present Moment
Alignment TRansformer (MATR) – a transformer-based architecture that leverages explicit
alignment for precise moment localization. MATR transforms the target video into a query-
aligned representation via a transformer framework to exploit finer-level correlation and
dependencies for precise moment localization.
6.4.1 MOTIVATION BEHIND MATR ARCHITECTURE
Video moments vary significantly in duration, motion patterns, and visual appearance, making
their retrieval inherently challenging. Additionally, preserving the correct temporal order of
events is crucial for accurate localization, as moments often involve complex interactions that
unfold over time. A robust Vid2VidMR model must, therefore, capture both high-level semantic
relationships and fine-grained frame dependencies to ensure precise alignment between the
query and target video.
To address these challenges, MATR incorporates an explicit dual-stage sequence alignment
strategy within a transformer-based encoder-decoder framework. This strategy enables MATR
to learn a query-aligned representation of the target video by combining the abstract representation
from the encoder with the refined fine-grained features from the decoder. In doing so, it facilitates
precise moment localization by leveraging both global semantic alignment and fine-grained
temporal dependencies between the query and target videos. Fig. 6.2 illustrates the overall
architecture of MATR, which we discuss in detail in the following.
6.4.2 ARCHITECTURE DETAILS
Input Representation. We uniformly sample frames every 2 seconds to obtain 𝑀and 𝑁frames
from the target and query videos, respectively. We encode these frames using a frozen CLIP [50]
(ViT-B/32) encoder along with a linear projection module. CLIP is applied separately to both
videos to obtain target video embeddings E𝑡∈R𝑀×𝑑and query video embeddings E𝑞∈R𝑁×𝑑.
The linear projection module is a two-layer perceptron, each with layer normalization and
dropout. It maps 512-dimensional CLIP embeddings to 𝑑-dimensional outputs. The resulting
projected features (E𝑡and E𝑞) are concatenated along the sequence length dimension to form the
input to the transformer encoder, denoted as E𝑐= [E𝑡; E𝑞] ∈R(𝑀+𝑁)×𝑑.

Figure 6.2: Moment Alignment TRansformer (MATR) Architecture. We represent the target video as
a query-aligned representation capturing the required correlation and dependencies with query video
at the abstract level (output of encoder) and finer level (output of decoder). These features are used to
extract foreground target frames, as well as localize the relevant moment in terms of start and end times
via two prediction heads. The alignment is computed using representations both before and after the
encoder (shown using blue dotted and red dashed lines, respectively). [Best viewed in color].
Encoder. The combined target and query video representation (𝐸𝑐) is processed through a
series of 𝑘standard Transformer [28] encoder layers, each comprising a multi-head self-attention
mechanism and a feed-forward network. Further, following the prior works [10, 210, 211], fixed
positional encodings are added to the input of each attention layer to preserve temporal order.
The encoder generates a fused representation [Eg
t ; Eg
q] of the target video conditioned on the
query video. Here Eg
t ∈R𝑀×𝑑and Eg
q ∈R𝑁×𝑑represent the target and query parts respectively.
Dual-stage Sequence Alignment. The transformer encoder computes effective features by
performing a joint understanding (or fusion) of target and query video frames. The final goal of
Vid2VidMR is to localize a moment in the target video that semantically matches the content of
the query video. In other words, we would prefer the features of the moment in the target to
align strongly with the features of the query video. Therefore, we perform dual-stage sequence
alignment, i.e, before and after the encoder. In our model, we incorporate several alignment
techniques—including soft-DTW [209], TCC [196], DropDTW [175], and OTAM [212], etc. to
enable robust temporal matching between query and target videos. While our framework
supports all these methods, we describe the alignment process here using soft-DTW for clarity.
Pre-fusion alignment: Before encoder fusion, given the target and query video feature sequences,

Pre-fusion alignment enhances the semantic representation of target and query video. These
enhanced representations are further processed by the encoder. The encoder outputs the fused
representation of the target and query.
Post-fusion alignment: Given the post-fusion target and query video feature sequences, Eg
t ∈

Post-fusion alignment refines target video features by leveraging fused query-target representa-
tions, ensuring fine-grained semantic matching for precise moment localization.
Decoder. The decoder in MATR further refines the target video representation by processing
aligned query-target features, enabling fine-grained temporal matching and enhancing frame-
level precision for accurate moment localization. To this end, the post-fusion alignment matrix
Apost defines a contiguous sub-sequence Eg
t [𝑠: 𝑒] which aligns best with the query video.
These aligned target features, capturing query-aligned fine-grained information, are passed
as input to the decoder for further refinement. The decoder consists of 𝑘Transformer layers,
each containing a multi-head self-attention layer, a multi-head cross-attention layer, and a
feed-forward network. The input to the decoder comprises fixed size 𝑙learnable query vectors
denoted by Q ∈[𝑞1, . . . , 𝑞𝑙] ∈R𝑙×𝑑. These queries guide the extraction of refined features
relevant to moment localization. As these queries are processed through each decoder layer,
they are refined to capture intricate temporal dependencies, with positional encodings applied
at each attention layer. The cross-attention layers enable interaction between Eg
t [𝑠: 𝑒] from the
encoder (which serve as keys and values) and representations of moment queries (which serve
as queries). The final decoder output, El
t ∈R𝑙×𝑑, provides a refined fine-grained set of target
video features conditioned on the query video.
Finally, the encoder and decoder representations are combined as Ef = [Eg
t ; El
t], making the
final target video representation query-aligned. The final query-aligned representation, combining
the abstract and fine-grained semantics, serves as input for the prediction heads.
Prediction Heads. The prediction heads consist of three (𝑐= 3) sequential convolutional layers
each having 𝑑, 1 × 3 kernels followed by a ReLU activation. Finally, a sigmoid activation function
is applied to produce the foreground predictions ˆ𝑓𝑖. The model is trained using the following
binary cross-entropy loss ℒ𝑓𝑔to distinguish foreground (relevant moment) from background

predictions:
ℒ𝑓𝑔= −

In addition to foreground classification, the boundary prediction head is designed to estimate
the start and end boundaries for the moment at every position. This head shares the same initial

Convolutional layers operate along the temporal axis over the concatenated encoder query-
conditioned and decoder-aligned target features. This preserves temporal continuity, enabling
fine-grained predictions without boundary artifacts.

6.4.3 SELF-SUPERVISED PRE-TRAINING STRATEGY
The video representation learning community has leveraged self-supervised pre-training tech-

be finetuned for enhanced performance on downstream tasks. In this work, we introduce a
self-supervised pre-training objective designed to improve temporal localization capabilities

This pre-training objective closely aligns with the task of Vid2VidMR. To enhance generalization
capabilities and encourage the model to become robust to variations in both appearance and
timing, we apply one of the following random augmentations to each of the query clips: reversing
frames, adding gaussian noise, slowing down or speeding up the action, thereby doubling the
number of pre-training samples. These augmentations introduce temporal and spatial variations,
enabling the model to learn diverse representations.
The overall pre-training loss, ℒ𝑝𝑡, combines the foreground-background classification loss,
the boundary prediction loss, pre-fusion and post-fusion alignment costs. The total loss is

6.5 DATASETS
We use the following two datasets to compare our proposed approach with existing Vid2VidMR
methods and other strong baselines:
6.5.1 ACTIVITYNET-VRL DATASET [173]
ActivityNet-VRL is the popular benchmark dataset shared by Feng et al. [173], based on the
ActivityNet video understanding benchmark [214] consisting of 200 action classes. The dataset
is split into 160 disjoint classes for training and 20 classes each for the validation and test splits.
Furthermore, the training set comprises 463K query-target video pairs, while the validation set
and the test set have 829 and 978 query-target video pairs, respectively. As shown in Table 6.1,

Table 6.1: Statistical Comparison Between ActivityNet-VRL and the Proposed SportsMoments Dataset.
While ActivityNet-VRL contains greater class diversity (160 training classes), SportsMoments offers sub-
stantially larger validation and test sets (10,000 pairs each versus 829/978), enabling more comprehensive
evaluation of model generalization capabilities.
this class-disjoint split ensures that models are evaluated on unseen activity categories, providing
a rigorous test of generalization capabilities. The temporal characteristics of ActivityNet-VRL
are detailed in Table 6.2, revealing that target videos have an average duration of 38.2 seconds
(median 29.3 seconds), with substantial variation ranging from very short clips (1.3 seconds)
to longer recordings (191.5 seconds). Query videos are significantly shorter, averaging just 8.2
seconds (median 7.2 seconds), with lengths varying from 0.5 to 57.2 seconds. This substantial
difference between query and target video durations highlights the challenging nature of the
temporal localization task, where models must identify short actions within much longer
contexts. The extensive training set, combined with diverse activity categories and varying video
lengths, makes ActivityNet-VRL a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating the generalization
capabilities of Vid2VidMR models across different types of human activities.
6.5.2 OUR PROPOSED SPORTSMOMENTS DATASET
One of the most promising applications for Vid2VidMR lies in the area of sports analytics.
Although there exist large-scale sports datasets such as Sports-1M [215], they are primarily
tailored for broader sports video classification tasks and do not contain fine-grained sports
actions and moments, such as “Cover Drive,” “Ducking a Bouncer,” “Goal Kick,” or “Penalty.”
Secondly, although ActivityNet-VRL covers a wide range of action categories (including sports
actions), it only encompasses broader categories such as “Playing Polo” and does not focus on
capturing higher-granularity events within a sport. Towards filling this gap, we introduce the
SportsMoments dataset, which consists of ∼770K query-target pairs annotated from 176.6 hours
of complete match footage of two of the most popular sports, viz. soccer and cricket. We obtain
a total of 80 full-length cricket and soccer match videos from YouTube. We then curate a list of
29 action classes comprising 13 soccer and 16 cricket actions, respectively. Given this list, we
employed two annotators, each with strong knowledge of these sports, to mark the start and
end timestamps for the specified actions in the videos. We split SportsMoments into training,
validation, and test sets. The training split consists of approximately 750K pairs spanning 16
classes, while the validation and test splits each contain 10K pairs, covering 4 and 9 classes,
respectively. Action classes are disjoint across the train, validation, and test sets, ensuring
no overlap. Additionally, each split includes cricket and soccer actions for a well-rounded
distribution. The statistics of the dataset are provided in Tables 6.1 and 6.2.
Additionally, we leverage the unlabeled videos from the Kinetics700 [165] dataset for
pre-training.

Figure 6.4: We visualize the distribution of a) target video durations and b) query video durations in our
proposed dataset, SportsMoments.
SPORTSMOMENTS DATASET CURATION
The construction of query and target videos follows a systematic process. Each annotated
segment in the dataset serves as a potential query video associated with its corresponding action
class. To create a target video, the annotated segment is offset by a duration ranging between 30
to 60 seconds on either side, i.e., both towards the start and end of the segment. Additionally, a
scaling factor of 25% of the annotated segment’s duration is applied to this offset to account for
variability in segment lengths.
During the annotation phase, to maintain data quality, we ensured that immediately
overlapping segments (representing the same action occurring within 2 seconds) are merged
into a single segment. Furthermore, since the dataset uses full-length matches for annotation,
the number of overlapping segments across different classes is negligible, ensuring that the
dataset remains clean and avoids ambiguities.
Annotation verification and agreement. Each annotator’s work was independently reviewed
by the other, and any segment on which the two disagreed was removed rather than adjudicated,
so the released dataset contains only mutually confirmed annotations. Of the candidate segments
produced in the first pass, 85.4% of cricket segments and 95.0% of soccer segments were confirmed.
The disagreements are highly structured. In cricket, classes defined by outcome, such as Four,
Six, Bowled and LBW, reach 99.5% agreement, whereas classes defined by batting technique,
such as Sweep Shot, Yorker and Cover Drive, reach only 69.3%; almost all disagreement falls
in the latter group. Outcomes are unambiguously signalled in broadcast footage through the
scoreboard or the umpire signal, whereas technique classes require discriminating visually
83


Video Action Classes
Figure 6.5: Frequency distribution of action classes in the SportsMoments dataset. The dataset comprises
a wide range of actions across the two sports, including frequent activities such as "header," “four," and
“throw," as well as less common ones like “red card" and “uppercut." The figure highlights the variation
in class occurrences, reflecting the diversity and imbalance in real-world sports scenarios.
similar strokes. This is precisely the fine-grained difficulty the dataset is intended to capture, and
it also locates where any residual label noise is concentrated. The higher soccer rate is consistent
with this explanation, since the soccer taxonomy is largely outcome-defined. We note that,
because the second annotator marked candidates as correct or incorrect rather than supplying
an independent label, the figures above are verification statistics rather than a chance-corrected
agreement coefficient such as Cohen’s 𝜅.
Guarding against query–target leakage. A query and its target are always drawn from
different source videos, so a target never contains the query segment itself and retrieval cannot
be solved by locating a copy of the query. In addition, the action classes in the train, validation
and test splits are disjoint, so no class seen during training appears at test time. Together these
two constraints ensure that the reported performance reflects generalisation to unseen actions
rather than memorisation of specific footage.
Each pair in the dataset consists of a short clip acting as a query video coupled with a longer
target video belonging to the same action class. For the creation of training pairs, we adopt a
strategy similar to that used in [173]. A query video can be paired with all target videos of the
same class. Unlike [173], which generates such exhaustive pairings on the fly, we precompute
and store all possible pairs during the dataset creation process.
84


For the test and validation splits, we curate a fixed set of 10,000 pairs for each split. These splits
are designed to ensure fair representation of all classes, thereby enabling balanced evaluation
across the dataset.
Compared to SportsMoments, ActivityNet-VRL has a greater variety of classes in the training
split, which supports more diverse class coverage. However, SportsMoments has significantly
more pairs overall in the validation and test splits, which provide a broader evaluation framework
for our tasks. The target videos in SportsMoments benchmark range from a minimum of 35.4
seconds to an upper bound of 208.8 seconds with the mean and median lengths being 98.3 and
98.2 seconds respectively. The query video is constrained to be atleast 1 second in duration as we
believe this to be the minimum duration in which an action/event can be depicted coherently.
Maximum length of a query video in our proposed dataset is 66 seconds consisting of overlapping
scenes of the same action including replays from different perspectives. Mean and Median of
the query videos are 6.0 seconds and 5.0 seconds, respectively. For ActivityNet-VRL, the training
split has 462,872 pairs covering 160 classes, while the validation and test splits have 829 and 978
pairs, respectively, each covering 20 classes.
We collected 64 cricket and 16 soccer full length matches collectively spanning 176.6 hours.
We were able to extract 4999 segments from all the matches, with the average length of each
segment being 6.0 seconds. Out of these 4999 segments 2925 fall under cricket and 2074
belong to soccer. Each segment is annotated with its start and end timestamp. The number of
segments per-class is shown in Figure 6.5. Table 6.1 shows a comparison between the proposed
SportsMoments and the repurposed ActivityNet [173].

YOUTUBE IDS USED IN SPORTSMOMENTS
We also release the YouTube IDs of videos used in our dataset, 80 in total as shown in Table 6.3.
For cricket, we have a total of 64 videos, 26 of which are full length twenty over games, 9 are fifty

match and 3 videos are that of a single day Test-match innings. The remaining 16 matches are
that of full length football matches from various leagues.
85


6.6 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS
6.6.1 BASELINES
We experiment with an extensive set of strong baselines grouped into four categories as follows.
FULLY-SUPERVISED VID2VIDMR METHODS
We compare with Vid2VidMR methods like CGBM [173], GDP [23], SRL [25], FFI+SRM [22],
SST [216] and Video-level match [173]. These approaches represent traditional fully-supervised
methods that have been specifically designed for video-to-video moment retrieval tasks. Many of
these methods rely on sophisticated attention mechanisms to establish correspondences between
query and target video features, but they often lack the explicit alignment strategies that our
approach incorporates. Their performance varies significantly based on the feature extraction
backbones and matching strategies employed, with more recent methods generally achieving
better results through improved feature representation learning. Further, Huo et al. [22] adapt
text-VMR methods like VSLNet [217], MABAN [218] and 2D-TAN [219] by replacing their text
encoders with a C3D [187] feature extractor to extract query video features. This adaptation
enables text-based methods to process video queries, providing a more direct comparison with
our approach. While these adaptations demonstrate reasonable performance, they often struggle
with effectively modeling the temporal dynamics present in video queries, as they were originally
designed for a different modality of text. This highlights the need for approaches like ours that
are specifically engineered to handle the unique challenges of video-to-video correspondence.
All of these models have been trained on ActivityNet-VRL.
VISION-LANGUAGE MODELS (VLMS)
VLMs like Video-LLaMa [220], Video-LLaVa [221], and TimeChat [222] have shown promising
results for multimodal text-video tasks like Video VQA, captioning, etc. These foundation
models leverage large-scale pre-training on diverse multimodal datasets to develop general
representations that can be applied to various downstream tasks with minimal adaptation.
Their ability to understand complex semantic concepts across modalities makes them attractive
candidates for video understanding tasks. Despite their impressive performance on various
video understanding tasks, these models face inherent limitations when applied to video-to-video
retrieval. The primary challenge stems from their architectural design, which is optimized for
text-video interactions rather than video-video comparisons that require fine-grained temporal
alignment capabilities. Unfortunately, they have not been pre-trained with video to video aligned
data. This lack of video to video pretraining significantly impacts their ability to perform well
on our task without substantial modification. To this end, we first represent the query video
using a state-of-the-art captioner, i.e., mPLUG-OWL [223]. This conversion from video to text
enables these text-centric models to process video queries indirectly. However, this approach
inevitably loses some of the rich temporal and visual information present in the original query
video, potentially limiting performance on tasks that require precise temporal alignment. Next,
86


the VLMs are zero-shot prompted to generate start and end moment timestamps given the query
caption and target video tokens.
TEXT-VMR METHODS
Moment-DETR [20], QD-DETR [21], CG-DETR [171], BM-DETR [201] and UniVTG [24] have
been originally proposed for text-VMR. These methods represent the current state-of-the-
art approaches for textual moment retrieval and serve as strong baselines when adapted to
video queries. Each of these approaches employs a different architectural design, with more
recent methods like UniVTG leveraging unified vision-text encoders for improved cross-modal
understanding, while earlier methods like Moment-DETR primarily rely on transformer-based
encoders with cross-attention mechanisms. We compare with five variants of these methods.
The zero-shot variant (a) uses caption from mPLUG-OWL [223] to represent the query video, and
leverages the pretrained checkpoints. This approach tests how well existing models can transfer
to our task without any task-specific training. Variants (b) and (c) both use a caption from
mPLUG-OWL [223] to represent the query video, but train a randomly initialized checkpoint
(variant b) or finetune the pre-trained checkpoint (variant c), respectively. These variants evaluate
whether task-specific training improves performance when using text representations of video
queries. Though textual representations capture semantic content, they inevitably lose important
visual and temporal nuances present in the original query videos, potentially limiting the upper
bound on performance for these text-based variants. Variants (d) and (e) are equivalent to (b) and
(c), where their CLIP text encoder is replaced by CLIP ViT/B-32 vision encoder, and therefore
take the query video directly as input along with the target video. These latter variants provide
the most direct comparison to our approach as they process video queries without converting to
text intermediaries.
IMAGE-VMR METHODS
Our work focuses on video moment retrieval using ‘video’ queries. As a single image query
may not effectively capture the temporal aspects of a video query, this comparison may not be
appropriate. The fundamental limitation here is that static images lack the ability to represent
dynamic actions and temporal evolution that characterize video content. This limitation becomes
particularly evident in scenarios involving complex activities with significant motion patterns
or when distinguishing between visually similar actions that differ primarily in their temporal
execution. However, we still design baselines by representing the video by its key-frame (more
precisely, the middle frame) using Text-VMR methods, namely Moment-DETR, QD-DETR, and
UniVTG. This simplified representation allows us to quantify the value of temporal information
in query videos compared to static image representations.
6.6.2 IMPLEMENTATION DETAILS
We choose a hidden dimension of 1024 with 𝑘= 4 layers in both encoders and apply a dropout
rate of 0.1 and 0.5 within the transformer and linear projection layers, respectively. Model
weights are initialized using Xavier initialization [224]. To optimize the model parameters, we

6.6.3 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION
Table 6.4 shows our main results where we compare MATR with fully-supervised Vid2VidMR
methods, VLMs, image-VMR methods, and five variants of each of the Text-VMR methods. As
mentioned before, these variants correspond to zero-shot, finetuned, or trained setups. As usual,
the trained setup implies random initialization, while the finetuned setup implies initialization
using a pre-trained checkpoint (different for each architecture). Query video can be represented
using text (T) captions or as the video (V) itself. For MATR, we show results with various video
feature extraction backbones and sequence alignment methods. Following previous work, we
report the standard mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) and Recall@1 IoU=0.5 metrics.
89


COMPARISON WITH FULLY-SUPERVISED VID2VIDMR METHODS
For a fair comparison, we directly use the reported results from original papers for fully-
supervised methods on ActivityNet-VRL. Consequently, we do not report their results on
SportsMoments. As shown in Table 6.4, MATR outperforms the best method, FFI+SRM, with an
11.4% gain in mIoU and achieves a 15.4% improvement in R@1 over GDP, which had the highest
recall. These results underscore MATR’s superior ability to accurately localize moments using
video queries, validating its effectiveness across diverse and challenging benchmarks.
COMPARISON WITH VLMS
Table 6.4 shows that our method MATR outperforms all four VLM-based baselines by a significant
margin. This performance gap indicates the limitations of general-purpose vision-language
models when applied to specialized temporal tasks without task-specific architectures. In
comparison, the best-performing baseline, TimeChat [222], achieves 26.4% mIoU and 23.8% R@1
on ActivityNet-VRL, and 22.6% mIoU and 21.3% R@1 on SportsMoments. The performance
difference between TimeChat and our method (33.7% mIoU difference on ActivityNet-VRL and
41.3% mIoU on SportsMoments) highlights the importance of architectures specifically designed
for temporal reasoning and alignment. Other methods, such as Video-LLaVA [221], Video-
LLaMA [220], and Video-LLaMA2 [226], perform significantly worse. Despite the impressive
general capabilities of these models in video understanding tasks, their performance on the
fine-grained temporal localization required for moment retrieval remains limited. These results
show that task-specific models for Vid2VidMR are significantly better.
COMPARISON WITH TEXT-VMR METHODS
Table 6.4 shows that amongst all the five variants, the finetuned variants are typically better
than the trained variants, i.e., variant (c) is better than (b), and (e) is better than (d). On both
ActivityNet-VRL and SportsMoments, BM-DETR achieves the best results among variants that
use video queries. However, our MATR model outperforms BM-DETR by 9.2% in mIoU and
5.7% in R@1 on ActivityNet-VRL. On SportsMoments, MATR shows a further improvement,
surpassing BM-DETR by 16.3% in mIoU and 16.4% in R@1. These gains highlight the superior
performance of MATR compared to the text-VMR methods when used with video query input.
When text captions are used to represent the query, we observe similar trends as in the video query
input case. Among the baselines, BM-DETR performs the best. However, MATR outperforms it
by 3.6% in mIoU and 4.2% in R@1 on ActivityNet-VRL. On SportsMoments, MATR achieves
3.9% higher mIoU and 8.7% higher R@1 than BM-DETR. These results demonstrate the superior
performance of MATR compared to the text-VMR methods when used with text query input for
Vid2VidMR. One surprising finding is that Moment-DETR and QD-DETR perform worse with
video queries compared to text queries, even after fine-tuning, especially for SportsMoments.
On the other hand, UniVTG and CG-DETR show expected trends, i.e., performance is better
with video queries than with text queries for these two models.
90


COMPARISON WITH IMAGE-VMR
Table 6.4 shows that the best-performing approach, UniVTG [24], achieves a mIoU of 38.8%
and 42.0% R@1 on ActivityNet-VRL and 34.6% mIoU and R@1 37.2% on SportsMoments. This
performance gap is particularly evident when comparing across different evaluation metrics and
datasets, highlighting the inherent limitations of image-based representations for temporal tasks.
Although this is comparable to previous fully supervised methods, it still lags significantly
behind our MATR. The substantial performance difference (21.3% mIoU and 17.4% R@1 on
ActivityNet-VRL, 29.3% mIoU and 24.1% R@1 on SportsMoments) demonstrates that video
queries contain crucial motion patterns and temporal context that static images simply cannot
capture. This further goes to show the importance of temporal dynamics captured by video
queries as compared to static image queries.
COMPARISON ON TEXT-VMR TASK
Experimental results in Table 6.5 demonstrate that our proposed model achieves competitive
performance on the Text-VMR task on the QVHighlights dataset, particularly excelling in
temporal boundary precision with the highest R1@0.7 score of 54.2%. While CG-DETR maintains
superiority in most metrics (R1@0.5, mAP@0.5, mAP@0.75, and average mAP), our approach
shows a notable 2.1% improvement over CG-DETR in R1@0.7, suggesting better precision in
temporal localization. The trade-off appears to be a slight decrease in mAP metrics compared
to CG-DETR, though our model still outperforms earlier approaches like Moment-DETR and
competes well with other recent methods.
ADVANTAGE OF DUAL SEQUENCE ALIGNMENT
Table 6.6 shows the impact of different alignment strategies in MATR on both datasets. We
evaluate the effect of incorporating alignment at the pre-fusion and post-fusion stages. The
pre-fusion alignment helps establish initial cross-modal correspondences, while post-fusion
alignment refines these relationships after contextual encoding. Our results indicate that the
combined use of both pre-fusion and post-fusion alignment achieves the best performance,
suggesting that alignment is important to ensure both before and after the encoder. This
dual alignment strategy enables the model to progressively refine cross-modal relationships
at multiple levels of abstraction. Models with alignment either before (pre) or after (post)
show moderate gains over neither. The incremental improvements demonstrate that each
alignment stage contributes uniquely to the model’s ability to localize moments. Removing post-
fusion alignment hurts more than removing pre-fusion alignment, suggesting that post-fusion
alignment is more important. This indicates that alignment after contextual encoding captures
more semantically meaningful relationships between modalities. Omitting alignment entirely
results in the lowest scores. The significant performance drop (3.5% mIoU on ActivityNet-VRL
and 7.1% mIoU on SportsMoments) when removing both alignment stages emphasizes their
complementary nature. This highlights the importance of dual alignment for effectively capturing
relevant temporal information for video query-based moment localization.

EFFECT OF PRE-TRAINING
We analyze the impact of pre-training and data augmentation on model performance in Table 6.7.
On ActivityNet-VRL, without pre-training, the model achieves 53.2% mIoU and 54.8% R@1.
Adding pre-training without augmentation yields 54.1% mIoU and 55.6% R@1, demonstrating
the benefit of leveraging large-scale video-text datasets prior to task-specific fine-tuning. The
best results are obtained by using pre-training with augmentation, leading to a significant boost
to 56.8 mIoU and 57.1% R@1, a 3.6% improvement in mIoU over the baseline. Similar trends are
observed on the SportsMoments dataset, where the full configuration achieves 59.2% mIoU and
56.5% R@1, confirming the generalizability of our approach across different datasets. During
augmentation for each sampled clip, one of the following transformations is applied: reversing
frames, adding Gaussian noise, slowing down the action, or increasing the action speed. These
augmentations enhance the model’s robustness to temporal variations and noise, which are
common challenges in real-world video understanding scenarios. The consistent performance
improvements across both datasets highlight the importance of our two-stage training strategy
in developing robust video moment retrieval systems.

fine temporal boundaries. The transformer decoder refines coarse alignments and models

matching, both are modest compared to the improvements from prediction heads. These results
emphasize the decoder’s critical role in transforming alignment signals into precise moment
predictions.
ABLATION ON ALIGNMENT LOSSES
MATR can incorporate different alignment methods. Table 6.9 compares the performance
of different alignment methods on both ActivityNet-VRL and SportsMoments datasets. Our
extensive ablation study comparing different alignment methods for MATR reveals that OTAM
achieves the best performance across both datasets, with substantial improvements of 7.8%
and 10.1% in R@1 over the baseline on ActivityNet-VRL and SportsMoments, respectively.
Temporal alignment is clearly beneficial, as all methods outperform the non-aligned baseline,
with sophisticated approaches like OTAM and DTW showing particularly strong gains. Among
the evaluated methods, OTAM consistently achieves the highest performance (58.2% mIoU,
58.7% R@1 on ActivityNet-VRL and 62.2% mIoU, 58.6% R@1 on SportsMoments), followed
closely by soft-DTW, which demonstrates the second-best results across both datasets. The
performance improvements from simpler alignment methods like TCC and LCSS are more
modest but still significant, confirming that the choice of alignment algorithm plays a crucial
role in moment retrieval performance.
Although OTAM attains the best accuracy, we retain soft-DTW as the reference configuration
throughout the remaining ablations. Soft-DTW is the method with which MATR was developed
and the one for which this chapter gives the full derivation, and every other ablation in this
section was run under it, so keeping it fixed leaves those comparisons mutually consistent.
Re-running the full suite under OTAM would be unlikely to change any of the conclusions, since
the two differ by a small and roughly constant offset. Soft-DTW is also preferable on its own
merits in some settings: its 𝛾-smoothed minimum aggregates over all alignment paths weighted
by cost rather than committing to a single best path, which yields smoother gradients when
frame-level similarities are noisy, and it is differentiable everywhere, which is the property that
allows it to serve as ℒpre
align and ℒpost
align and on which the dual-stage design depends.
94


ABLATION ON FEATURE BACKBONES
The ablation study in Table 6.10 on feature backbones for MATR demonstrates that more advanced
video feature extractors consistently yield better performance across both evaluated datasets.
Specifically, Slow-Fast achieves the highest performance with 54.7% mIoU and 55.9% R@1 on
the ActivityNet-VRL dataset, and 58.3% mIoU and 55.4% R@1 on the SportsMoments dataset,
showing a clear advantage over traditional architectures like ResNet and I3D. Interestingly,
CLIP also performs strongly, particularly on the first dataset, suggesting that vision-language
pre-training provides valuable transferable features for moment retrieval tasks. Surprisingly, the
combination of CLIP and Slow-Fast features doesn’t lead to further improvements and actually
performs worse than either backbone individually, indicating potential feature redundancy or
integration challenges that warrant further investigation in future work.
ROBUSTNESS STUDY OF MATR
From the robustness study presented in Table 6.11, we observe that our MATR demonstrates
varying degrees of resilience to different types of noise. The model shows remarkable stability
against Gaussian noise and speed variations, with performance drops of only 1.0% and 3.5%
in mIoU, respectively, on ActivityNet-VRL, suggesting effective handling of these common
real-world variations. Frame reversal causes a more significant performance decline (14.1%
mIoU drop on ActivityNet-VRL), indicating that temporal direction remains an important signal
for the model. This comprehensive analysis demonstrates that while MATR is robust to several
common video distortions, it appropriately remains sensitive to semantic query relevance.
The asymmetry between speed variation and frame reversal follows directly from the design.
DTW exists to absorb local accelerations and decelerations, so a query performed faster or
slower than the target is handled by the warping path; frame reversal, by contrast, violates
the monotonicity constraint that all valid alignments enforce and cannot be represented at all.
Both perturbations also appear as augmentations during self-supervised pre-training, which
further explains the speed robustness. Perspective mismatch between query and target was
not isolated under controlled perturbation, but it is present throughout the evaluation rather
than absent from it: SportsMoments is built from broadcast footage, and its longer query clips
consist of overlapping views of the same action including replays from different camera angles.
The qualitative results below make the same point on ActivityNet-VRL, where MATR localises
correctly under significant viewpoint change, across multi-shot sequences with varying camera
angles, and between different environments.
QUALITATIVE RESULTS
We present moment retrieval results on four samples in Fig. 6.6, where we compare our proposed
approach to variant (e) (refer to Sec.6.6.1 for details) of the four best-performing baselines. Our
proposed MATR model exhibits the highest overlap with the ground truth for the given query,
accurately identifying the correct temporal boundaries. Among the baseline methods, BM-DETR
and UniVTG achieve strong overlap; however, they are still outperformed by our approach.
