EFFICIENCY ANALYSIS
Since the dual-stage design invokes sequence alignment twice, once before and once after the
encoder, and each stage is 𝑂(𝑀𝑁) in the query and target sequence lengths, it is reasonable to
ask whether alignment dominates the cost. Table 6.12 shows that it does not. All measurements
are taken on an NVIDIA A6000 GPU with batch size 1, averaged over 100 inference runs. MATR
requires 15.4 GFLOPs and 58.6M parameters, with 53.8 ms inference and 2.5 GB peak memory.
It is therefore cheaper than BM-DETR (23.8 GFLOPs, 81.3M parameters, 71.5 ms, 3.8 GB) and
CG-DETR (18.1 GFLOPs, 58.4 ms, 3.1 GB), both of which are single-stage, while achieving 9.2%
higher mIoU than BM-DETR. Against BM-DETR specifically, MATR uses 27.9% fewer parameters,
35.3% fewer FLOPs and 34.2% less memory, and is 24.7% faster. The reason alignment does not
dominate is that it operates on sequences of at most a few hundred sampled frames rather than
on raw video.
COST AS A FUNCTION OF SEQUENCE LENGTH
Table 6.13 reports how accuracy and cost vary with the frame sampling rate, which controls
the sequence length 𝑀for a target video of fixed duration. Inference time ranges from 30.1 ms
at 1 frame/4s to 178.9 ms at 2 frames/s, and peak memory from 1.7 GB to 8.1 GB. The default
rate of 1 frame/2s sits at the accuracy knee: doubling the rate to 1 frame/1s raises inference
time by 77.7% and memory by 76.0% for a 0.3% change in mIoU, and denser sampling degrades
accuracy through redundancy between highly correlated adjacent frames. Sparser sampling
at 1 frame/4s costs 5.5% mIoU on ActivityNet-VRL, indicating that critical motion cues are
missed. Under a fixed 48 GB budget at the default resolution, this corresponds to a maximum
processable target duration of roughly 8 minutes at the default rate, extending to roughly 33
minutes at 1 frame/4s. All ActivityNet-VRL (max 191.5 s) and SportsMoments (max 208.8 s)
96


videos are therefore comfortably within range at the default setting.
FAILURE CASE ANALYSIS
To characterise the practical limitations of MATR, we inspected the 100 test samples with the
lowest mIoU and identified three dominant failure modes. First, perceptually different action
representations, where the same action appears visually distinct across query and target, for
example normal versus seated rope skipping, producing large cross-instance feature distances
that weaken alignment. Second, viewpoint variation, where query and target are recorded from
substantially different perspectives, such as a third-person view against a first-person view.
Third, speed variation, where the action is performed at noticeably different speeds, for example a
normal-speed pole vault against a slow-motion replay, causing soft-DTW to match incorrectly
stretched sub-sequences. These modes are consistent with the robustness results reported above
and indicate that viewpoint-invariant and speed-invariant representations remain the principal
open problem for this task.
CROSS-DOMAIN GENERALISATION
To assess robustness to cross-domain appearance shift, we converted the query videos in the
test set into cartoon/anime-style footage using DCT-Net, leaving the target videos unchanged.
MATR achieves 38.3% mIoU under this setting, despite the frozen CLIP encoder never having
seen cartoon-style imagery during training. This indicates that the model retains meaningful
localisation ability even under a large stylistic gap between query and target, while the drop
relative to the unperturbed setting shows that appearance-invariant and domain-adaptive
representation learning remains an important direction for future work.
6.7 SUMMARY
In this chapter, we introduced MATR, a well-motivated approach for Vid2VidMR. By combining
the abstract representation of the encoder and the refined fine-grained features of the decoder
conditioned on aligned query features, MATR effectively captures both semantic and temporal
information, enabling accurate moment localization. Our self-supervised pre-training strategy
further improves the model initialization and boosts performance. Extensive experiments showed
that MATR outperforms strong baselines on both ActivityNet-VRL and our novel SportsMoments
dataset, showcasing its effectiveness in tackling this challenging task. Although MATR excels at
localizing moments using video queries, future research can extend it to multimodal queries
and develop more efficient architectures to enhance scalability for Vid2VidMR.
The following chapter presents the conclusion of our work, discusses its limitations, and
outlines potential directions for future research.
97


7
Conclusions and Future Scope
This thesis advances query-guided video understanding across three complementary levels, object,
relationship, and moment, enabling machines to answer where, how, and when in open-world
videos with minimal supervision. The motivation for this work is twofold. First, real-world
deployments often encounter long-tailed and continuously evolving visual concepts, making
exhaustive annotation and closed-set training impractical. Second, users increasingly demand
targeted, example-driven retrieval, locating a specific object, interaction, or moment, without
manually scanning long videos. To address these challenges, this thesis proposes query-driven
grounding frameworks that leverage minimal supervision, temporal reasoning, and query-based
generalization to perform robust video understanding.
7.1 SUMMARY AND DISCUSSION
At the object level (understanding “where”), this thesis advances video object grounding through
two complementary frameworks designed to localize novel objects in videos using minimal
supervision. For the one-shot scenario, we introduce QDETRv (Query-Guided DETR for Videos, a
transformer-based architecture that extends DETR with temporal modeling capabilities. QDETRv
employs (i) query–context fusion, where query features are integrated with contextual video
frames to enable effective temporal reasoning; (ii) a memory module that maintains temporal
consistency across frames; and (iii) an unsupervised pretraining strategy using synthetic object
trajectories with recurrent queries and feature reconstruction objectives. These components
collectively enhance temporal modeling and generalization, allowing QDETRv to achieve
substantial gains over competitive baselines. We further extend one-shot object grounding
to few-shot object grounding. To this end, we proposed the Temporal Object-Aware Vision
Transformer, which leverages large-scale, language-aligned, pre-trained vision encoders. This
framework integrates semantic knowledge from vision–language models like OWL-ViT to enable
effective knowledge transfer and robust object generalization over time. The model introduces a
filtering mechanism that propagates high-confidence object features across frames, enabling
efficient progression and reduced noise. It further incorporates feature interaction between
current frames and past detections to maintain temporal coherence without explicit object-tube
proposals, which are often computationally expensive and require task-specific training. Parallel
classification and detection heads are optimized for few-shot settings, achieving state-of-the-art
performance on FSVOD-500, FSYTV-40, VidOR, and VidVRD benchmarks. Together, these
contributions advance the understanding of “where” objects are located in videos.
At the relationship level (understanding “how”), this thesis extends individual object grounding
98


to model interactions between objects through few-shot referring relationship grounding in
videos. The task is formulated as inference over a 𝑇-partite random field, where 𝑇denotes
the number of frames in the video, and each vertex represents candidate subject–object pairs.
Meta-learned potentials and query-conditioned translational embeddings are introduced to
encode relational semantics and capture meaningful interactions between entities. The model
achieves improved subject–object–predicate localization accuracy, with ablation studies validating
the complementary roles of Global Semantic Aggregation (GSA), which captures long-range
contextual dependencies, and Local Localization Aggregation (LLA), which focuses on fine-
grained spatial cues and temporal consistency. Despite these advances, grounding dynamic
predicates with complex temporal evolution remains challenging, and performance is influenced
by the quality of subject–object proposals and the computational cost of message passing across
frames. Future extensions could involve joint optimization of proposals and relationships using
tubelet-level candidates, sparse or neural message passing for improved scalability, and end-
to-end integration with open-vocabulary detectors to enhance better generalization to unseen
subjects and objects. Moreover, extending the formulation to include temporal-tubes of objects
for predicates would naturally connect “how” and “when,” enabling richer and temporally
grounded scene graphs that capture evolving visual relationships over time.
At the moment level (understanding when), the thesis introduces the Moment Alignment
Transformer (MATR) for video-to-video moment retrieval. MATR aligns a short query clip with
a longer target video using dual-stage sequence alignment, employing differentiable Dynamic
Time Warping before and after fusion to refine temporal correspondences. The model achieves
notable gains in mIoU and Recall@1 on ActivityNet-VRL and SportsMoments, demonstrating
the importance of explicit sequence alignment and task-aligned self-supervised pretraining.
However, computational cost increases with sequence length, and the current design also focuses
on single-moment retrieval, overlooking multi-event queries. We further extended MATR to
support text-to-video moment retrieval and achieved comparable, if not better, performance to
the state-of-the-art methods. Future extensions could include hierarchical coarse-to-fine retrieval
for efficiency, non-monotonic or skip-aware alignment for flexibility, and multimodal query
fusion with text or audio to improve disambiguation and generalization. The restriction to single-
moment retrieval deserves particular comment, since it follows the benchmark specification
rather than a limitation of the architecture: Vid2VidMR as formalised in the literature, and as
evaluated on ActivityNet-VRL, assumes one ground-truth moment per query–target pair. Two
routes would lift it. The lighter of the two requires no retraining at all: a long target video
could be partitioned into overlapping chunks, with MATR run on each chunk against the same
query and predictions above a confidence threshold pooled across chunks to yield multiple
moments. This would be parallel across chunks and would bound peak memory by chunk length
rather than video length, at the cost of truncating moments that straddle a chunk boundary
and requiring per-chunk scores to be normalised before pooling. The principled route would
instead replace the single boundary head with a set-prediction head over the 𝑙learnable queries
that MATR already carries, trained with Hungarian matching against a variable number of
ground-truth moments; this would also require a benchmark providing multi-moment ground
truth, which neither dataset used here currently does.
99


7.1.1 TRANSFERABILITY OF COMPONENTS ACROSS TASKS
As the three tasks were formulated and solved independently, experimentation revealed that two
components were transferable across them. Language-aligned visual backbones from the CLIP
family were useful at two of the three levels, as OWL-ViT in the few-shot object framework and
as a frozen CLIP encoder in MATR; in both cases the gain came from semantic priors that the
task-specific training data could not supply, in the object setting for separating visually similar
categories and in the moment setting as the strongest single backbone on ActivityNet-VRL.
Object-level temporal state propagation also transferred: the recurrent-query memory module
in QDETRv and the confidence-thresholded feature propagation in the few-shot framework
are the same principle at different levels of maturity, the second having been designed directly
from the limitations of the first. Two components did not transfer, for structural rather than
empirical reasons. The dual-stage alignment losses presuppose two sequences to align, whereas
in the object and relationship tasks the query is a single image or a triplet, so there is no second
sequence and the loss has no natural analogue. Belief propagation over the 𝑇-partite field is
likewise tied to the pairwise subject–object candidate structure of the relationship task and has
no counterpart in single-entity grounding. Cross-task joint training was not attempted, and
closing that gap is precisely the aim of the unified architecture outlined in Section 7.3.
Overall, these contributions collectively demonstrate that temporal reasoning and query
conditioning enable flexible, open-world grounding in videos under minimal supervision,
forming a scalable foundation for structured video understanding.
7.1.2 TRAINING COST AND REPRODUCIBILITY
The computational requirements of each contribution are distributed across the implementation
sections of the individual chapters, which makes the overall cost hard to assess; we therefore
summarise them here. QDETRv is pretrained on synthetic trajectories over UCF101 together
with image-level pretraining on ImageNet, and is trained on three NVIDIA RTX A6000 GPUs
using Adam with a learning rate of 1e-5 for 200 epochs at a batch size of 350, initialised from an
ImageNet-pretrained ResNet-50. The few-shot object framework builds on OWL-ViT ViT-L/16
used off the shelf, and converges in approximately four days on two A6000 GPUs using AdamW
with weight decay 0.01 and a cosine schedule with linear warmup. The few-shot relationship
framework is meta-trained episodically on support sets rather than pretrained on a separate
corpus. MATR is pretrained on unlabeled Kinetics-700 with a frozen CLIP ViT-B/32 encoder
and trained on two A6000 GPUs with AdamW at a learning rate of 1e-4. For reproducibility,
the category-disjoint VidOR and VidVRD splits used throughout the object and relationship
chapters are themselves a contribution of this thesis and are released alongside the code. For
SportsMoments, the source video identifiers are listed in this chapter, and we additionally release
the segment-level timestamp annotations and the pre-computed validation and test pair lists,
which is what makes the benchmark reproducible in practice rather than merely re-collectable.
100


7.2 POTENTIAL APPLICATIONS
The proposed methods have broad applicability across domains where query-based grounding
can replace exhaustive manual analysis of videos.
In sports analytics, these models can
automatically extract highlights, track players, and retrieve tactical plays from exemplar clips.
In manufacturing, they can detect defective components or rare fault patterns from a few
visual examples, enabling rapid adaptation to new products. In education and skill learning,
exemplar-based moment retrieval supports indexing and feedback in instructional videos,
helping systems identify critical steps such as “when the weld begins” or “when the coffee is
poured.” In healthcare, temporal grounding can detect and timestamp key events such as falls
or procedural deviations from limited annotated data. In security and forensics, query-based
localization allows analysts to locate specific objects or interactions across long surveillance
videos with minimal supervision.
Together, these applications highlight the versatility of query-driven grounding systems,
where users specify what to find via queries rather than predefined class names, enabling adaptive
and efficient video understanding across diverse domains.
7.3 FUTURE DIRECTIONS
In this section, we outline research directions that extend and broaden query-guided video
understanding.
7.3.1 UNIFIED “ANY-QUERY, ANY-GROUNDING”
Our current methods have shown remarkable performance on three video grounding tasks:
QDETRv handles image queries for object grounding, the relationship grounding framework
processes visual triplets, and MATR operates on video/text queries for temporal grounding.
One of the promising future directions of this thesis is to develop a unified architecture that can
handle multiple query modalities such as image, video, and text, and generate diverse grounding
outputs such as boxes, masks, relation spans, and moments through a shared backbone with
task-agnostic query-target fusion. This would involve using a modality-agnostic encoder, similar
to CLIP or Video-OWL, to process different types of queries while encoding the target video
with temporal tokens. Dual-stage alignment could then be used to produce query-aligned
target features that are consistently reused across all prediction heads. Building on this shared
representation, the system could support multiple heads for detection, segmentation, relation
trajectories, and moment boundaries, effectively extending the capabilities of architectures like
QDETRv and MATR. Finally, incorporating class-agnostic matching and open-vocabulary priors,
together with few-shot prototype heads and temporal filtering, would enable robust handling
of uncertainty and open-set scenarios. However, we note that such an extension is non trivial
and would require require additional rigorous experimental studies involving architectural
integration, joint training, and appropriate multi-task evaluation, which is beyond the scope of
the current thesis.
101


7.3.2 EFFICIENCY AND STREAMING VIDEO
A key challenge moving forward is to scale grounding to live video streams and long-duration
content while operating under tight computational budgets. One promising direction is to explore
streaming transformers that maintain causal state caching, along with keyframe-adaptive token
sparsification and memory-augmented decoders that reuse high-confidence tokens across time
to enable low-latency updates. We have taken initial steps in both directions: language-guided
temporal token pruning reduces the number of video tokens a VideoLLM must process [230],
and a strictly causal state-space core supports streaming video anomaly detection [231]. These
ideas naturally extend the object-aware temporal propagation strategies used in few-shot VOD.
Another opportunity lies in using lightweight prefilters that identify candidate windows based
on query-conditioned signatures before applying more expensive grounding modules.
A related and largely untested axis is generalisation to fully unstructured, in-the-wild footage.
The benchmarks used in this thesis are less curated than they may appear: VidOR is built from
user-generated social-media video with handheld camera motion and clutter, FSVOD-500 and
FSYTV-40 draw from YouTube, and SportsMoments consists of full-length broadcast matches
with panning, cuts, replays and on-screen graphics. Category shift is also tested by construction
throughout, since every benchmark uses class-disjoint train and test splits. What remains
genuinely untested is egocentric video, where the camera is itself the actor and ego-motion,
hand occlusion and objects leaving frame are constant, together with untrimmed long-form
video at the scale of hours rather than the few-minute targets considered here. We would expect
the few-shot object framework to degrade most gracefully, since its open-vocabulary priors are
domain-robust and confidence-thresholded propagation naturally discards unreliable frames,
and QDETRv to suffer most, given its documented sensitivity to occlusion and small objects.
The relationship framework would be limited by proposal quality under ego-motion rather than
by the random field itself, and MATR by the growth of the retrieval space in long-form video
rather than by camera motion. Egocentric benchmarks such as Ego4D, whose episodic-memory
and moment-queries tasks map closely onto the where and when formulations of this thesis, are
the natural setting in which to test these expectations.
7.3.3 DIRECTIONS WITH VIDEO LLMS
Contemporary Video LLMs show considerable promise, yet they still fall short of specialized
grounding methods when precise localization and timestamping are required, especially
in the absence of strong video-to-video alignment or object-aware temporal modeling; a
survey of current VideoLLM benchmarks and evaluation practices is presented in [232]. A
promising direction is to adopt a hybrid approach, in which Video LLMs handle intent parsing,
disambiguation, and iterative refinement of the user’s query, while the actual fine-grained
grounding is delegated to dedicated modules, such as object-aware few-shot VOD and dual
alignment Vid2VidMR proposed in this thesis. Making such hybrid systems trustworthy also
requires verifying that the evidence a Video LLM cites in its answers is actually grounded in the
video [233].
In summary, this thesis establishes a foundation for query-driven, open-world video ground-
ing and highlights the path toward unified, efficient, systems that can seamlessly process long,
102


dynamic, and complex videos. Continued research along these directions promises not only
better models but also more practical video understanding for real-world applications.
